Boundary-Border Extensions of the Kuratowski Monoid
Mark Bowron

Abstract
\vskip-12.0pt The Kuratowski monoid 𝐊 \mathbf{K} is generated under operator composition by closure and complement in a nonempty topological space. It satisfies 2 ≤ | 𝐊 | ≤ 14 2\leq|\mathbf{K}|\leq 14 . The Gaida–Eremenko (or GE) monoid 𝐊𝐅 \mathbf{KF} extends 𝐊 \mathbf{K} by adding the boundary operator. It satisfies 4 ≤ | 𝐊𝐅 | ≤ 34 4\leq|\mathbf{KF}|\leq 34 . We show that when | 𝐊 | < 14 |\mathbf{K}|<14 the GE monoid is determined by 𝐊 \mathbf{K} . When | 𝐊 | = 14 |\mathbf{K}|=14 if the interior of the boundary of every subset is clopen, then | 𝐊𝐅 | = 28 |\mathbf{KF}|=28 . This defines a new type of topological space we call Kuratowski disconnected . Otherwise | 𝐊𝐅 | = 34 |\mathbf{KF}|=34 . When applied to an arbitrary subset the GE monoid collapses in one of 70 70 possible ways. We investigate how these collapses and 𝐊𝐅 \mathbf{KF} interdepend, settling two questions raised by Gardner and Jackson [ 59 ] . Computer experimentation played a key role in our research. C source can be found at https://www.mathtransit.com/c.php . Keywords : border, boundary, closure, complement, frontier, Hasse diagram, interior, operator monoid, poset, semilattice. MSC : 54A05, 54G05, 06F05 Published version [ 20 ] : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.topol.2023.108703



1
Introduction

Kelley’s
classic textbook
[
98
]
posed the following result
to generations of students as the
kuratowski closure and complement problem

[
104
,
180
]
:

If
A
is a subset of a topological space, then at most
14
sets can be
constructed from
A
by complementation and closure. There is a subset
of the real numbers
(
with the usual topology
)
from which
14
different
sets can be so constructed.

It now usually goes by the name
Kuratowski
closure-complement theorem

(or
problem
, or
14
-set theorem
).
Modern treatments of the subject can be found in Gardner and Jackson

[
59
]
(we refer to it/them as GJ; some familiarity with GJ is
recommended but not necessary to understand the present paper) and
Sherman’s

informative Monthly article
[
147
]
.
1
1

1

For more references see

Bowron’s
list at
https://www.mathtransit.com/cornucopia.php
.

In this paper we study variants of
Kuratowski’s
theorem involving boundary and
border.

Anatolii Gol’dberg
posed the closure-complement-boundary problem in a course on
discrete mathematics at the University of Lviv in
1972
(personal communication,

A. Eremenko
,
6
Dec
2019
).
First-year undergraduates
Yurii Gaida
and
Alexandre Eremenko
solved it
independently.
2
2

2

The problem occurred to Gol’dberg while browsing Zarycki’s Ph.D.
thesis
[
180
]
to prepare for a talk in his memory. Eremenko heard of it
through word of mouth as he was not attending Gol’dberg’s course at the time.

They showed that an arbitrary subset generates at most
34
distinct sets and found
all inclusions that hold in general among them.
The
Ukrainian Mathematical Journal
published their work

[
57
]
(we refer to it/them as GE).
3
3

3

This was also published
as a Monthly problem in 1986
[
27
]
.
In
1982
Soltan
[
156
]
solved the version that assumes a general closure
operator (see GJ, Figure 2.2 and Section 4.2 for further discussion).
The closure-complement-boundary problem also appears
in
[
29
,
59
,
100
,
114
]
.
In 1927 Zarycki
[
180
]
replaced closure in the closure-complement problem
with various other operators including boundary and border.
GE’s result is for
Boolean algebras with a closure, i.e., closure algebras.
It implies the corresponding topological result
[
122
]
.
Various boundary operators also appear in

[
3
,
6
,
42
,
55
,
56
,
70
,
77

,

,

,

,

]
.
For a philosophical take on the boundary concept see Varzi
[

]
.

recently posted their previously unpublished diagram of inclusions at

https://www.math.purdue.edu/˜eremenko/dvi/table1.pdf
.

GJ found connections between the monoid of operators generated under composition
by closure and complement and its action on individual subsets.
As we show in Section
5
, the boundary and border operators help settle
two questions they raised in this area.

and
Eremenko’s

the foundation for later sections.

2
The Closure-Complement-Boundary Theorem

Most of the definitions below are used throughout the paper.
The first few only apply when no further information is given.
For the reader’s convenience a brief glossary of symbols is provided in
Table
1
.

2.1
Notation and terminology.

The pair
(X,\mathcal{T})
denotes an arbitrary nonempty topological space.
The symbols
A,A_{j}
denote arbitrary subsets of
X
.
The set
(2^{X})^{2^{X}}
of all set operators on
2^{X}
is denoted by
\mathbf{O}
.
The symbol
\mathcal{O}
denotes an arbitrary subset of
\mathbf{O}
and
o,o_{j}
arbitrary
set operators in
\mathbf{O}
.

The set
\mathbf{O}
forms a monoid under
composition with the identity operator
\mathsf{id}
as the identity element and

(o_{1}o_{2})A:=o_{1}(o_{2}A)
for all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{O}
and
A\subseteq X
.
For
p\in\mathbf{O}
we define
p\mathcal{O}:=\{po:o\in\mathcal{O}\}
and

\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptp:=\{op:o\in\mathcal{O}\}
.
For
A\subseteq X
we define
\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptA:=\{oA:o\in\mathcal{O}\}
.

The relations
o_{1}=o_{2}
,
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
are
satisfied
by
A
if and only if

o_{1}A=o_{2}A
,
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
, respectively, and by
(X,\mathcal{T})
if and only if all
A\subseteq X

satisfy them.

The relation
\leq
is a partial order on
\mathbf{O}
.
4
4

4

As usual, by

partial order
we mean a reflexive, transitive, asymmetric binary relation.

The join
(\vee)
and meet
(\wedge)
of
\mathcal{O}
exist and satisfy
\left(\bigvee_{\!\mathcal{O}}o\right)\!A\,=\,\bigcup_{\mathcal{O}}oA
and
\left(\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}o\right)\!A\,=\,\bigcap_{\mathcal{O}}oA
.
Let
\mbox{Ord}(\mathcal{O}):=\{(o_{1},o_{2})\in\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{O}:o_{1}\leq o_{2}
for all
(X,\mathcal{T})\}
.
5
5

5

o_{1}\leq o_{2}\mbox{ for all }(X,\mathcal{T})\iff o_{1}\leq o_{2}\mbox{ in }(\mathbb{R},\mbox{usual topology})
by
McKinsey–Tarski

[
17
,
122
]
.

Operators
o_{1},o_{2}
are
disjoint
if
o_{1}\wedge o_{2}
is the

zero operator

0
defined by
0A:=\varnothing
.
6
6

6

We use dashed edges to
represent disjointness in poset diagrams (see Figure
1
).

The
one operator

1
equals
a\hskip 0.5pt0
where

A\longmapsto
a

X\setminus A
is the complement
operator on
2^{X}
.
7
7

7

The set
\mathbf{O}
is a Boolean algebra under

\{\vee,\wedge\}
.
For details see Sherman
[
147
]
.

The
difference

o_{1}\setminus o_{2}
equals
o_{1}\wedge ao_{2}
.

An operator
o
is
open
if
oA
is open for all
A\subseteq X
.
Closed operators are defined similarly.

Let
b
be topological closure,
i:=aba
interior,
f\hskip-1.0pt:=b\wedge ba
boundary (aka
frontier), and
g:=\mathsf{id}\wedge ba
border.
Define

\mathbf{K^{0}}:=\{\mathsf{id},

b,

i,

bi,

ib,

bib,

ibi\}
,

\mathbf{F^{0}}:=\{0,

f\hskip-1.0pt,

i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

f\hskip-0.5ptb,

f\hskip-1.0pti,

f\hskip-0.5ptbi,

f\hskip-1.0ptib\}
,

\mathbf{G^{0}}:=\{g,

bg,

f\hskip-0.5ptbg\}
,

\mathbf{KF^{0}}:=\mathbf{K^{0}}\cup\mathbf{F^{0}}
,

\mathbf{FG^{0}}:=\mathbf{F^{0}}\cup\mathbf{G^{0}}
,

\mathbf{KFG^{0}}:=\mathbf{KF^{0}}\cup\mathbf{G^{0}}
, and

\mathbf{S}:=\mathbf{S^{0}}\cup a\mathbf{S^{0}}
for each
\mathbf{S^{0}}
above.

GJ call
\mathbf{K}
the
Kuratowski monoid
and its elements
Kuratowski
operators.

It will be shown below that
\mathbf{KF}
is generated by
\{a,b,f\hskip-1.0pt\,\}
, hence we call
\mathbf{KF}
the

Gaida–Eremenko monoid
and its elements

Gaida–Eremenko operators
.

Kuratowski
[
104
]
proved that the four
closure axioms

b(A_{1}\cup A_{2})=bA_{1}\cup bA_{2}
,

b\leq\mathsf{id}
,
bb=b
,
b\varnothing=\varnothing
imply
\mathbf{K}
is the submonoid of
\mathbf{O}
generated
by
\{a,b\}
.
8
8

8

This follows from the identities
bababab=bab\iff bibi=bi\iff ibib=ib
(right- and left-multiply by
a
).
The literature usually credits Hammer
[
77
]
with proving that the axioms

b\varnothing=\varnothing
and
b(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\subseteq bA_{1}\cup bA_{2}
are not necessary for
\{a,b\}
to
generate
14
distinct operators but Chittenden
[
38
]
proved this

19
years before with
bb=b
weakened to
bbb=b
.

The axiom
b(A_{1}\cup A_{2})=bA_{1}\cup bA_{2}
implies that every
o\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
is isotone.
It follows that we can left- or right-multiply both sides of any equation or inequality
in
\mathbf{KFG}
by an operator in
\mathbf{KFG}
(reversing order when necessary) with one exception:
since
f\hskip-1.0pt
and
g
are neither isotone nor antitone, we generally cannot left-multiply
inequalities in
\mathbf{KFG}
by operators in
\mathbf{FG}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

In Sections
3
-
4
we find all quotients

\mathbf{KF}/\textstyle{\sim}
and
\mathbf{KF}/\textstyle{\sim}_{A}
where
o_{1}\sim o_{2}\iff o_{1}=o_{2}
and

o_{1}\sim_{A}o_{2}\iff o_{1}A=o_{2}A
.
For brevity we suppress the quotient notation and refer to equivalence classes by their
constituent operators.
9
9

9

This shorthand is
permissible since the projections from
\mathbf{KF}
onto
\mathbf{KF}/\textstyle{\sim}
and

\mathbf{KF}/\textstyle{\sim}_{A}
are isotonic monoid homomorphisms.
We

apply it to other monoids as well.

GJ call
K((X,\mathcal{T})):=|\mathbf{K}|
the
K-number of

(X,\mathcal{T})
,
k(A):=|\mathbf{K}A|
the
k-number
of

A
, and
k((X,\mathcal{T})):=\max\{k(A):A\subseteq X\}
the
k-number of

(X,\mathcal{T})
.
We denote the
\mathbf{KF}
analogues by
K_{\!f}
and
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
.

Note that
k((X,\mathcal{T}))\leq K((X,\mathcal{T}))
.
GJ call spaces satisfying
k((X,\mathcal{T}))=K((X,\mathcal{T}))

full
.
If
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))=K_{\!f}((X,\mathcal{T}))
we call
(X,\mathcal{T})

completely full
.

GJ call spaces with
K
-number

14

Kuratowski spaces
.
Subsets with
k
-number

14
were given the name
Kuratowski
14
-set
by Langford

[
107
]
.
10
10

10

The first
14
-set appeared in Zarycki
[
180
]

(Kuratowski used two complementary sets with
k
-number

12
to prove
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{K})
).
Since finding a
14
-set is half the closure-complement problem, Zarycki’s name could
have been added to it.
In the Closing Remarks we name a certain class of problems

Kuratowski–Zarycki
problems.

We naturally call the
\mathbf{KF}
analogues
Gaida–Eremenko spaces
and

34
-sets
.

Kuratowski
[
104
]
calls operators in
\mathbf{K^{0}}

even
and
a\mathbf{K^{0}}

odd
due to the number of times “
a
” must appear in any product representing
them.
Canilang et al
.

[
29
]
observed that these terms
extend naturally to
\mathbf{KF}
in nonempty spaces since
\mathbf{KF^{0}}\cap a\mathbf{KF^{0}}=\varnothing

(o_{1}(\varnothing)=\varnothing

and
ao_{2}(\varnothing)=X
for all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}})
.

The
duals
of
o,\mathcal{O}
are
d(o):=aoa,d(\mathcal{O}):=\{d(o):o\in\mathcal{O}\}
.
Respectively, the
left dual
,
right dual
, and
dual
of the
equation
o=p
(inequality
o\leq p
) are:
ao=ap

(ap\leq ao)
,
oa=pa

(oa\leq pa)
,
and
aoa=apa

(apa\leq aoa)
.

2.2
Gaida and Eremenko’s theorem.

The following are obvious consequences of the definition of the dual.

Lemma 1
.

(i)

d(o_{1}o_{2})=d(o_{1})d(o_{2})
,

(ii)

a(d(o))=oa
,

(iii)

d(d(o))=o
.

Parts (ii)-(iii) imply the operator
a
can be moved across
o
in either direction by
changing
o
to
d(o)
.
The reader is advised to memorize this rule for
o\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
.

Corollary 1
.

The dual of an operator in
\mathbf{K^{0}}
is obtained by interchanging
i
and

b
in its product representation.
Thus
d(bib)=ibi
,

d(\mathsf{id})=\mathsf{id}
,

d(ib)=bi
,
etc
.

Proof.

Have
d(b)=i
and
d(i)=b
.
Apply Lemma
1
(i).
∎

Lemma 2
.

b\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pta
.

Proof.

b\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=b(b\wedge ba)=b\wedge ba=ba\wedge baa=f\hskip-1.0pta
.
∎

The Hasse diagram of
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{K})
dates back to Kuratowski
[
104
]
.
Its extension to
\mathbf{KF}
forms the bedrock of this paper.

Theorem 1
.

(Gaida and Eremenko
[
57
]
)

Figure
1
(i)
and its left dual represent
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
.

Proof.

GE proved all edges hold.
Computer results complete the proof.
11
11

11

All C programs for this paper can be found
at
https://www.mathtransit.com/c.php
.

∎

Lemma 3
.

All entries in Table
2
are correct
.

Proof.

Suppose
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
.
If
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}},o_{2}\in a\mathbf{K^{0}}
then
iA\subseteq o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\subseteq aiA
, hence
iA=\varnothing
.
If
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}},o_{2}\in\mathbf{FG^{0}}
then
iA\subseteq o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\subseteq f\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq aiA
.
If
o_{1}\in a\mathbf{FG^{0}},o_{2}\in\mathbf{FG^{0}}
then
iA\subseteq ao_{2}A\subseteq ao_{1}A\subseteq f\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq aiA
.
If
o_{1}\in a\mathbf{KFG^{0}},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KFG^{0}}
then
abA\subseteq o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\subseteq bA
, hence
bA=X
.
The inequations hold since
X\neq\varnothing
.
∎

Corollary 2
.

o_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}ao_{2}
and
ao_{3}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}o_{4}
for all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}

and
o_{3},o_{4}\in\mathbf{KFG^{0}}
.

Proof.

Table
2
implies
o_{1}X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}ao_{2}X
and
ao_{3}\varnothing\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\subseteq}}}o_{4}\varnothing
.
∎

Corollary 3
.

(i)

|\mathbf{KF^{0}}\!A|=17\implies|\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA|=34
,

(ii)

|\mathbf{KFG^{0}}\!A|=20\implies|\mathbf{KFG}A|=40
.

Proof.

(i) Suppose
|\mathbf{KF^{0}}\!A|=17
.
Then
|(a\mathbf{KF^{0}})A|=17
.
Since
|\mathbf{K^{0}}\!A|=7
, Table
2
implies
o_{1}A\neq o_{2}A
for all
o_{1}\in a\mathbf{KF^{0}}
,

o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
Conclude
|\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA|=34
.
The proof of (ii) is similar.
∎

Lemma 4
.

(i)

bi(A\cup B)\subseteq b(iA\cup B)
,

(ii)

i(bA\cap B)\subseteq ib(A\cap B)
.

Proof.

(i) Since
i(A\cup B)\setminus bB
is an open set contained in
A
,
i(A\cup B)\subseteq iA\cup bB
.
Hence
bi(A\cup B)\subseteq b(iA\cup bB)=biA\cup bB=b(iA\cup B)
.
(ii) is the dual of (i).
∎

Lemma 5
.

(i)

i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pto=0\mbox{ for each }o\in\{b,i,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
,

(ii)

gi=ig=0
,

(iii)

f\hskip-1.0ptg=bg
,

(iv)

ibg=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.

Proof.

(i)
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptb=i(bb\wedge bab)=ib\wedge ibab=ib\wedge abib=0
,
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pti=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptai=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptba=0
,
and
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6ptb\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
.
(ii)
gi=\mathsf{id}(i)\wedge ai(i)=i\wedge ai=0
and

ig=i(\mathsf{id}\wedge ai)=i\wedge iai=i\wedge abi=0
.
(iii)
f\hskip-1.0ptg=bg\wedge bag=bg\wedge aig=bg
.
(iv)
ibg\leq i(bg\vee bga)=ib(g\vee ga)=ib\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
By Lemma
4
(ii) we have
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i(f\hskip-1.0pt\wedge b)\leq ib(f\hskip-1.0pt\wedge\mathsf{id})=ibg
.
∎

Proposition 1
.

All entries in Table
3
are correct
.

Proof.

Lemma
5
,
b\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt
, and
\{b,i,f\hskip-1.0pt,g\}0=0\{b,i,f\hskip-1.0pt,g\}=\{0\}
imply the zero
entries.
All other nontrivial entries with
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
and
o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
are implied by

ibi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=ibib\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=ib\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
and/or idempotence of
b
,
i
,
bi
, and
ib
.
The remaining nontrivial entries with
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
follow from
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
,

f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptb=f\hskip-0.5ptb
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbib=f\hskip-1.0ptib
and their right duals (see GE for proofs).
All entries involving
g
are easy consequences of Lemma
5
and the results
above.
∎

\cdots\leq ghg\leq gh\leq g\leq\mathsf{id}\leq h\leq hg\leq hgh\leq\cdots

.

GE’s set

The existence of a subset that distinguishes every unequal pair in
\mathcal{O}

is guaranteed by the disjoint union construction but authors traditionally supply an

\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 2
.

(Gaida and Eremenko
[
57
]
)

The monoid
\mathbf{KF}
of operators generated by
\{a,b,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
in a
given topological space has cardinality at most
34
and
|\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA|=34
for some

A
in some space
.

The set
A
above also satisfies
|\mathbf{KFG^{0}}\!A|=20
for we have

gA=(\mathbb{Q}\cap(2,3))\cup\{2,4,5,6\}
,
bgA=[2,3]\cup\{4,5,6\}
, and

f\hskip-0.5ptbgA=\{2,3,4,5,6\}
.
Thus
|\mathbf{KFG}A|=40
by Corollary
3
(ii).

2.3
The partial orders on
\mathbf{KF}
and

\mathbf{KFG}
.

We begin this subsection with several basic results.
The next lemma is clear.

Lemma 6
.

If
o_{1}\leq ao_{2}
and
o\leq o_{1}\vee o_{2}
then
oA\subseteq o_{1}A\iff oA\subseteq ao_{2}A
.

We verified by computer that the following lemma gives all decompositions of the form

o_{1}=o_{2}\vee o_{3}
in
\mathbf{KFG^{0}}\setminus\{0\}
.
It is often combined with Lemma
6
.

Lemma 7
.

The operands of each join below are disjoint
.

\begin{array}[c]{l@{\hspace{4pt}}l@{\hspace{4pt}}l@{\hspace{4pt}}l}\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&b=f\hskip-1.0pt\vee i=f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee ib\emph{,}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&bib=f\hskip-1.0ptib\vee ib\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 4.0&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&bi=f\hskip-1.0pti\vee i=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\vee ibi\emph{,}\\
\emph{(iv)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=g\vee ga\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 4.0&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(v)}\hskip 3.0&bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\emph{,}\\
\emph{(vi)}\hskip 3.0&bg=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 4.0&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(vii)}\hskip 3.0&ai=g\vee a\emph{,}\\
\emph{(viii)}\hskip 3.0&\mathsf{id}=g\vee i\emph{.}\\
\end{array}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

Part (viii) is clear.
The decomposition
1=i\vee g\vee ga\vee ia
implies
f\hskip-1.0pt=b\wedge ba=a(ia\vee i)=g\vee ga
,

ai=g\vee ga\vee ia=g\vee a
, and
b=aia=f\hskip-1.0pt\vee i
.
To get the other decompositions right-multiply
b=f\hskip-1.0pt\vee i
by
b,

ib,

i,

bi,

f\hskip-1.0pt,

i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

bg
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{20pt}}l
@{\hspace{20pt}}l@{\hspace{14pt}}l}\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&ib\setminus bi=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\emph{,}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&bib=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\vee bi\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 20.&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&ib\setminus ibi=ib\wedge bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\emph{,}\\
\emph{(iv)}\hskip 3.0&bi\setminus ib=f\hskip-0.5ptb\wedge f\hskip-1.0pti=f\hskip-1.0ptib\wedge f\hskip-0.5ptbi\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 20.&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(v)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee f\hskip-1.0pti\emph{,}\\
\emph{(vi)}\hskip 3.0&b=bg\vee bi\emph{.}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 14.0\end{array}

Proof.

(i)
ib\setminus bi=ib\wedge iba=i(b\wedge ba)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
(ii) Left-multiply
ib\leq(ib\setminus bi)\vee bi=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\vee bi
by
b
.
(iii) By (ii),
ib\wedge biba=abia\wedge(bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pta\vee bia)=ib\wedge bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
(iv) By
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{K^{0}})
,
ai=bai
, and Lemmas
1
(ii) and

7
(ii)-(iii),
f\hskip-0.5ptb\wedge f\hskip-1.0pti=(b\wedge aib)\wedge(bi\wedge ai)=bi\wedge aib=(bib\wedge aib)\wedge(bi\wedge aibi)

=

f\hskip-1.0ptib\wedge f\hskip-0.5ptbi
.
(v) By (i) and Lemmas
6
and
7
,
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt\setminus i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt\wedge(aib\vee bi)=f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee f\hskip-1.0pti
.
(vi)
b=b(\mathsf{id})=b(g\vee i)=bg\vee bi
.
∎

Proposition 2
.

Let
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3})
be any permutation of
f\hskip-1.0ptib,

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,

f\hskip-0.5ptbi
.

Then
(i)
\pi_{1}\setminus\pi_{3}=\pi_{2}\setminus\pi_{3}
.
Also
,

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\hspace{8pt}}l}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-0.5ptbi\setminus f\hskip-1.0ptib=(ib\wedge bi)\setminus ibi\emph{,}\\
\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-1.0ptib\setminus f\hskip-0.5ptbi=bib\setminus(ib\vee bi)\emph{,}\\
\emph{(iv)}\hskip 3.0&\pi_{1}\leq\pi_{2}\vee\pi_{3}\emph{,}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 8.0&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(v)}\hskip 3.0&\pi_{1}A\subseteq\pi_{3}A\iff\pi_{2}A\subseteq\pi_{3}A\emph{,}\\
\emph{(vi)}\hskip 3.0&\pi_{1}A=\varnothing\implies\pi_{2}A=\pi_{3}A\emph{,}\\
\emph{(vii)}\hskip 3.0&\pi_{1}A\subseteq a\pi_{3}A\implies\pi_{1}A\subseteq\pi_{2}A\emph{.}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Proof.

(i)–(iii) Lemma
7
implies
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\setminus f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\wedge ib=(bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\setminus i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\wedge ib=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\wedge(ib\wedge bi)\leq(ib\wedge bi)\setminus ibi=ib\wedge f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\setminus f\hskip-1.0ptib
.
Conversely, Lemma
8
(ii) implies
(ib\wedge bi)\setminus ibi\leq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\wedge(ib\wedge bi)
.
This proves (ii) and the case
\pi_{3}=f\hskip-1.0ptib
in (i).
(iii) and
\pi_{3}=f\hskip-0.5ptbi
follow by right duality.
Suppose
\pi_{3}=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
The proven cases imply
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\setminus f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
and
f\hskip-1.0ptib\setminus f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
Thus
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib
and
f\hskip-1.0ptib\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbi
, giving us the result.
(iv) By (i),
\pi_{1}=(\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2})\vee(\pi_{1}\setminus\pi_{2})=(\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2})\vee(\pi_{3}\setminus\pi_{2})\leq\pi_{2}\vee\pi_{3}
.
Clearly
\mbox{(i)}\!\implies\!\mbox{(v)}\!\implies\!\mbox{(vi)}
and
\mbox{(iv)}\!\implies\!\mbox{(vii)}
.
∎

Parts (ii) and (iii) imply the following corollary.

Corollary 5
.

(i)
biA\cap ibA\subseteq ibiA\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptibA
,

(ii)
bibA\subseteq biA\cup ibA\iff f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.

Lemma 9
.

The following hold for all
A\subseteq X
.

\begin{array}[c]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}lcr@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq aA\emph{,}&&\emph{(iv)}\hskip 3.0&oA\subseteq biA\implies oA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\mbox{ for }o\in\{f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-1.0ptib,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}\emph{,}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-0.5ptbA\subseteq bibA\iff bibA=bA\emph{,}&&\emph{(v)}\hskip 3.0&oA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbA\implies oA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptibA\mbox{ for }o\in\{f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-0.5ptbi,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}\emph{,}\\
\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&oA\subseteq ibA\implies oA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\mbox{ for }o\in\{f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}\emph{,}&&\emph{(vi)}\hskip 3.0&oA\subseteq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies oA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\mbox{ for }o\in\{f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0ptib\}\emph{.}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

(i) Left-multiply by
i
to get
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A\implies i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq iA\implies i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\implies i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A
then substitute
aA
for
A
.
(ii) Apply
b=f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee ib
.
(iii) Since
o\wedge(ib\setminus bi)=0
we get
oA\subseteq biA
.
The result follows by Lemma
6
.
(iv)–(vi) Apply Lemma
6
.
∎

Definition 1
.

Let
P
be a partial order on a set
S
.

An ordered pair
(x,y)
in
(S\times S)\setminus P
is a
potential cover

of
P
if and only if
P\cup\{(x,y)\}
is a partial order.
We call the set of potential covers the
extender
of
P
,
denoted

\mbox{Ext}(P)
.

For brevity we define
\mbox{Ext}(\mathcal{O}):=\mbox{Ext}(\mbox{Ord}(\mathcal{O}))
for each
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathbf{O}
.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
P\subseteq Q
are partial orders on a finite set
S
.

Then

P=Q\iff\emph{Ext}(P)\cap Q=\varnothing.

Proof.

This holds since the poset under set inclusion of all partial orders on
S
is graded by
cardinality (see Lemma 2.1 in Culberson and Rawlins
[
46
]
).
∎

The next proposition implies GE’s
34
-set in
\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
.
It is sharp up to Proposition
2
(v).

Proposition 3
.

A GE
34
-set
A
satisfies
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
if and only if

o_{1}A\nsubseteq o_{2}A
for all
(o_{1},o_{2})\in\{(f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt)
,

(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-0.5ptbi)
,

(f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0ptib)\}\cup(\{f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\}\times\{\mathsf{id},a\})
.

Proof.

Suppose
A
is a GE
34
-set.
The “only if” holds by Theorem
1
.
Conversely, suppose
A
satisfies the condition.
By Figure
1
(iii), up to left duality we have
\mbox{Ext}(\mathbf{KF})=\bigcup_{j=1}^{7}S_{j}
where

S_{1}=\{(o,ao):o\in\{i,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\}\}\cup\{(ab,b)\}
,

S_{2}=\{(\mathsf{id},bib),

(ibi,\mathsf{id})\}
,

S_{3}=\{(i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,\mathsf{id}),

(i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,a),

(f\hskip-0.5ptb,bib)\}
,

S_{4}=\{(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,aib),

(f\hskip-0.5ptbi,aib),

(f\hskip-1.0pti,aibi)\}
,

S_{5}=\{(f\hskip-1.0ptib,a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptbi),

(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptib),

(f\hskip-0.5ptbi,a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\}
,

S_{6}=\{(f\hskip-1.0ptib,bi),

(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi),

(f\hskip-1.0ptib,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt),

(f\hskip-0.5ptbi,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\}
, and

S_{7}=\{f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\}\times\{\mathsf{id},a\}
.
Let
(o_{1},o_{2})\in\mbox{Ext}(\mathbf{KF})
.
Claim
o_{1}A\nsubseteq o_{2}A
.
This holds for
S_{1}
since
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\implies o_{1}A=\varnothing
.
It holds for
S_{2}
since
A\subseteq bibA\implies bibA=bA
(left-multiply by
b
) and
ibiA\subseteq A\implies ibiA=iA
(left-multiply by
i
).
It holds for
S_{3}
by Lemma
9
(i)–(ii),
S_{4}
by
Lemmas
6
-
7
,
Proposition
2
(v), and
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
,
S_{5}
by
Proposition
2
(vii), and
S_{6}
by
Lemma
9
parts (iv),(vi) and
Proposition
2
(v).
The hypothesis covers
S_{7}
.
The result follows by Theorem
1
and Lemma
10
.
∎

Proposition 4
.

Figure
1
(ii)
and its left dual represent
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KFG})
.

Proof.

All three edges with
g
as an endpoint are clear.
Left-multiply
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=ibg\leq bg
and
g\leq f
by
b
to get
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq bg\leq f
.
Right-multiply
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq b
by
g
to get
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq bg
.
Have
f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg\setminus ibg\leq f\hskip-1.0pt\setminus i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
The other edges hold by Theorem
1
.
We verified by computer that no further edges hold.
∎

2.4
Basic relationships.

This subsection lays the foundation for Section
4
.

Lemma 11
.

In Figure
1
(i),
each of the six edge equations in

(\mathbf{K^{0}}\setminus\{\mathsf{id}\})A
is equivalent to one of the nine edge equations in

(\mathbf{F^{0}}\setminus\{f\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\})A
.

Among the latter
,
only
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
fails to imply any equation in
\mathbf{K^{0}}\!A
.

Specifically
,
we have

(i)

bibA=biA\iff ibiA=ibA\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
,

(ii)

bibA=ibA\iff f\hskip-1.0ptibA=\varnothing
,

(iii)

ibiA=biA\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbiA=\varnothing
,

(iv)

bA=ibA\implies f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbA=f\hskip-1.0ptibA\iff bibA=bA
,

(v)

iA=biA\implies f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-0.5ptbA\implies f\hskip-1.0ptiA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\iff ibiA=iA
,

(vi)

f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies(bibA=bA\mbox{ and }ibiA=iA)
.

The following five non-edge equations in
\{f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}A

each imply at least one edge equation in
(\mathbf{K^{0}}\setminus\{\mathsf{id}\})A
.

(vii)

f\hskip-0.5ptbA=(f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\mbox{ or }f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA)\implies bibA=bA
,

(viii)

f\hskip-1.0ptiA=(f\hskip-1.0ptibA\mbox{ or }f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA)\implies ibiA=iA
,

(ix)

f\hskip-0.5ptbA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies(bibA=bA\mbox{ and }ibiA=iA)
.

We also have

(x)

f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq X
,

(xi)

bA=X\iff iA=a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff biA=ai\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff ibiA=abi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
,

(xii)

f\hskip-0.5ptbgA=\varnothing\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbgA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff bgA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies bibA=bA
.

Proof.

(i) Left-multiply
bibA=biA
by
i
and
ibiA=ibA
by
b
to get the first equivalence.
The second one holds since
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\implies ibA\subseteq biA\implies ibA=ibiA\!\implies\!i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ibA\subseteq biA\!\implies\!i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
.
(ii) Apply
bib=f\hskip-1.0ptib\vee ib
.
(iii) is the dual of (ii).
(iv) The equivalence holds by Lemma
7
(i)–(ii).
The implications hold by Lemmas
7
(i),

8
(iii), and
9
(ii).
(v) is the dual of (iv).
(vi) Have
f\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
by Lemma
7
(iv)–(v).
It follows that
bA\setminus bibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbA\subseteq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq bibA
and

ibiA\setminus iA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\subseteq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq aibiA
.
(vii) Apply Lemma
9
(ii).
(viii) is the dual of (vii).
(ix)
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA=f\hskip-0.5ptbA
by Lemma
8
(iii).
Apply (iv) and (v).
(x)
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=X
implies the contradiction
\varnothing=if\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptA=iX=X
.
(xi) Each implies
bA=X
since
o_{1}A=ao_{2}A\implies X=o_{1}A\cup o_{2}A\subseteq bA
for all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
Apply
b=f\hskip-1.0pt\vee i
to get
bA=X\implies iA=a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
.
Left-multiply by
b
and
i
to complete the proof.
(xii) The equivalence holds by Lemma
7
(v)–(vi).
The implications hold by
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KFG})
and
Lemma
8
parts (i) and (v).
∎

Definition 2
.

We call any set
C\subseteq\{\{o_{1},o_{2}\}:o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{O}\}
a
collapse
of

\mathcal{O}
.

A subset
A

satisfies

C
[
on

\mathcal{O}
]
16
16

16

We
often leave out “on
\mathcal{O}
” when the context is clear.
if and only if for
all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{O}
,

o_{1}A=o_{2}A\iff\{o_{1},o_{2}\}\in C
.

For
(X,\mathcal{T})
to
satisfy

C
,
replace
o_{1}A=o_{2}A
with

o_{1}=o_{2}
.

Satisfaction of a partial order
P
on
\mathcal{O}
by a space or subset is defined
similarly.

If some subset in some topological space satisfies a collapse
C
on
\mathcal{O}
we
call
C
a
local collapse of

\mathcal{O}
.

If some space satisfies
C
on
\mathcal{O}
we call
C
a
global
collapse of

\mathcal{O}
.

Local and global orderings are defined similarly.
17
17

17

Previous authors have used
various names for collapses and orderings.
The term “collapse” doubtlessly derives from the graphical collapsing that occurs when
comparable nodes merge in a Hasse diagram.
GJ refer to local collapses specifically as
equivalence patterns
.
McCluskey et al
.

[
120
]
call local orderings

properties
and global orderings
universals
.
Staiger and Wagner
[
158
]
call local orderings of
\mathbf{K^{0}}

Kuratowski lattices
.

We call a collapse
closed
if it contains the pair
\{\mathsf{id},b\}
and

open
if it contains
\{\mathsf{id},i\}
.

Every global collapse or partial order is local but the converse is not true in general.

Lemma 12
.

The collapse of
\mathbf{F^{0}}
that
A
satisfies is determined by the collapse

\{\{o_{1},o_{2}\}\in C_{1}\cup C_{2}\cup C_{3}:o_{1}A=o_{2}A\}
where
C_{1}
is the set of
all nine edge pairs in
\mathbf{F^{0}}\setminus\{f\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}
in
Figure
1
(i),

C_{2}=\{\{f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-1.0pti\},

\{f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\},

\{f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\},

\{f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-1.0ptib\},

\{f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}\}
,
and
C_{3}=\{\{f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\},

\{f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\},

\{f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0ptib\}\}
.

Proof.

Since
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\wedge f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
it follows that every equation in
\mathbf{F^{0}}\!A
involving an operator
in
\{0,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
is equivalent to some combination of edge equations in

\mathbf{F^{0}}\!A
.
Every such combination is determined by the nine edge equations in

(\mathbf{F^{0}}\setminus\{bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\})A
since
f\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
,
f\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
, and
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
by
Lemma
7
(iv)–(v).
Every incomparable pair in
\mathbf{F^{0}}\setminus\{0,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
is in
C_{2}\cup C_{3}
.
∎

Proposition 5
.

(Kleiner
[
100
]
)

A Kuratowski
14
-
set
A
is a GE
34
-
set if and only if at most one
of the following inclusions holds
:

f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptibA
,

f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
,

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\{f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0ptib\}
. If two of the inclusions hold then
|\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptA|<3

by Proposition
2
(v).
Conversely suppose
|\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptA|=3
.
Since
|\mathbf{K^{0}}\!A|=7
, Proposition
2
(vi) and
Lemmas
11
-
12
imply
|\mathbf{F^{0}}\!A|=10
.
By Corollary
3
we conclude
|\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA|=34
.
∎

Corollary 6
.

(Kleiner
[
100
]
)

If
A
is a Kuratowski
14
-
set and
biA\subseteq ibA
then
A
is a GE

34
-
set
.

Proof.

Apply Corollary
5
and Proposition
5
to

ibA\cap biA=biA\nsubseteq ibiA
and
bibA\nsubseteq ibA=ibA\cup biA
.
∎

Kleiner’s paper concludes by exhibiting a GE
34
-set
A
in
\mathbb{R}
with the novel
property that all
34
sets in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
are pairwise nonhomeomorphic.

3
The Gaida–Eremenko Monoid

In this section we find all global collapses and orderings of
\mathbf{KF}
and
\mathbf{KFG}
.

3.1
The monoid
\mathbf{KF}
.

GJ proved (see Theorems 2.1 and 2.10) that
\mathbf{K^{0}}
has:
(i) six global collapses, each of which extends uniquely to
\mathbf{K}
, and
(ii)
30
local collapses, one of which extends to two local collapses of
\mathbf{K}
and the
other
29
of which extend uniquely to
\mathbf{K}
(see Tables
4

and
8
).

It is surprising the local collapses of
\mathbf{K^{0}}
were not studied exhaustively until the

2000
s.
Chapman
[
35
]
studied a coarser equivalence on
2^{X}
.
In the widely-read
American Mathematical Monthly
, Chapman
[
36
]

gave necessary and sufficient conditions for each of the
\binom{7}{2}
possible
equations in
\mathbf{K^{0}}\!A
.
18
18

18

Chapman wrote both papers as an undergraduate student
(personal communication, H. W. Gould,
1999
).

With hindsight, the question jumps out: Which combinations of these equations occur?
It evidently escaped notice for over
40
years.
In the next section we show that they extend to
62
local collapses of
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
and
70

local collapses of
\mathbf{KF}
.

McCluskey et al
.

[
120
]
proved that
\mathbf{K^{0}}
has six
global and
49
local orderings.
The latter result was also found independently by Staiger and Wagner

[
158
]
.
19
19

19

Their unpublished
2010
conference paper was
uploaded to ResearchGate in late
2021
by Staiger.
It appears that it and the papers by McCluskey et al
.

(2007)
and GJ
(2008)

were all written independently of one another.

It turns out that
\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
, and
\mathbf{KF}
have
66
,
274
, and
496
local orderings,
respectively (see Theorem
8
).

Calling
|\mathbf{K}|
the
Kuratowski number
of
(X,\mathcal{T})
, Chagrov
[
34
]
was
the first author to exhibit the six global collapses of
\mathbf{K}
.
20
20

20

Chagrov omitted
proof that no further ones exist (for the proof see GJ).
Though there is no citation to confirm it, Kleiner
[
100
]
may have inspired
Chagrov’s work in this area.
Langford
[
107
]
showed that
A
is a Kuratowski
14
-set if
and only if five independent inclusions in
\mathbf{K^{0}}\!A
all fail.
Kleiner considered the five cases when each holds individually for all
A\subseteq X
.
They reduce to these three: (i) partition or discrete, (ii) OU, EO, or discrete,
(iii) ED, EO, or discrete.
For each, Kleiner gave a correct upper bound for
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))
and an example claimed to
realize this bound, but only one was entirely correct.
The family of closures of singletons in the OU example is misidentified as a base, which
instead yields an EO space, and the English version of the journal forgets to mention the
set
\{a,b\,^{\prime}\!,c\}
that generates
20
distinct subsets.
The ED example fails because it is not a topological space.
A subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is defined to be closed if and only if it is of the form

U\cup V
where
U
is finite and
V
is any collection of lines parallel to the

x
-axis.
The set
\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}(\{(n,n)\}\cup\{(x,m):x\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{N}^{+},m\neq n\})=\{(n,n):n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\}
is not closed under this definition.
Leaving
V
out gives us the ED space
(\mathbb{R}^{2},
cofinite topology) but it only has

k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
-number

4
.

Neither Chagrov nor GJ named all six space types per se; for brevity we do
so using Table
4
.

Lemma 13
.

The equations in each part below are equivalent
.

(i)

bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,\,\,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!0,\,\,f\hskip-1.0ptib\!=\!f\hskip-0.5ptbi
,

(iii)

f\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,\,\,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!0,\,\,bib\!=\!bi
,

(ii)

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-1.0ptib,\,\,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-0.5ptbi,\,\,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\!=\!0,\,\,f\hskip-1.0ptib\!=\!0,\,\,bib\!=\!ib
,

(iv)

i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-0.5ptb,\,\,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-1.0pti,\,\,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,\,\,f\hskip-1.0pt\!=\!0,\,\,b\!=\!i
.

Proof.

(i)
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0\implies f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\implies f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0ptibg=f\hskip-0.5ptbig=0
by
Proposition
2
(vi) and Lemmas
7
(v)
and
5
(iv).
(ii)
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0ptib\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-1.0ptib=0\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib=0\iff bib=ib
by right duality, part (i), and Lemma
7
(ii).
(iii) Apply Lemma
7
(iv) and Lemma
11
(i).
(iv)
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-0.5ptb\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti=f\hskip-0.5ptb=0\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\iff f\hskip-1.0pt=0\iff b=i
by right duality,

\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
, Lemma
8
(iii), and Lemma
7

parts (i) and (iv).
∎

Theorem 3
.

\mathbf{KF}
has exactly seven global collapses.
Figure
3
displays the six nonempty ones
.

Hence the only possible
K_{\!f}
-number
s are
:

4,10,16,20,22,28,34
.

Proof.

We verified by computer that seven exist (see Proposition
6
)
and claim no further ones do.

Since
X\neq\varnothing\implies i\neq 0
we have
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
for all
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}},o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
by
Table
2
.
Moreover,
\mathbf{KF^{0}}\cap a\mathbf{KF^{0}}=\varnothing
.
Hence our task is limited to finding all possible equations in
\mathbf{F^{0}}
for each
of the six global collapses of
\mathbf{K}
(see Table
4
).

Case

1
.
(|\mathbf{K^{0}}|=7
and
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\neq 0)

Have
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\neq i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\neq 0
.
Hence
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
Have
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\neq f\hskip-1.0pt
by Figure
2
.
Thus by
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
,
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
for all
o_{1}\in\{i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip 1.0pt\}
,
o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}\setminus\{i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip 1.0pt\}
.
By Lemma
13
(i)–(ii),
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
, and Figure
2
,

o_{1}\neq o_{2}
for all
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathop{\downarrow}\{f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
.
Conclude
|\mathbf{F^{0}}|=10
.

Case

2
.
(|\mathbf{K^{0}}|=7
and
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0)

Lemma
13
and Figure
2
imply the collapse of

\mathbf{F^{0}}
in Figure
3
(i).

All other cases are covered by Table
5
.
∎

K_{\!f}
-number

28
defines a special type of disconnected Kuratowski space we call

Kuratowski disconnected
(KD).
Note that
\mathbf{K^{0}}
and
\mathbf{F^{0}}
are order isomorphic in KD, EO, and discrete spaces.

Proposition 6
.

By Table
6
,
each GE monoid occurs in a unique space
of minimal cardinality up to homeomorphism
.

Bases for these spaces appear below
.

GE

\{w\},\{x,y\},\{w,x,y,z\}

KD

\{v\},\{w\},\{v,w,x\},\{y,z\}

ED

\{x,y\},\{x,y,z\}

OU

\{x\},\{y\},\{x,y,z\}

EO

\{x\},\{x,y\}

P

\{x,y\}

D

\{x\}

The number of nonhomeomorphic partition spaces on
n
points is one less than the number
of partitions of
n
(
1+1+\dots+1
corresponds to the discrete space).
This is sequence
A000065
in the OEIS
[
153
]
.
Besides the “all
1
’s” sequence
A000012
, no other
column in Table
6
appears in the OEIS.

Proposition 7
.

GE spaces satisfy
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KF})
.

Proof.

Let
X
be GE and
S_{1},\dots,S_{7}
be the sets in the proof of
Proposition
3
.
By the arguments in that proof, we have
o_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}o_{2}
for
(o_{1},o_{2})\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{3}
.
Right duality, Proposition
2
(v), and right-multiplication
by
i
yield
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbi\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbi\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-0.5ptbi
,

f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq a\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq\mathsf{id}\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq i\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq a
, and
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq a\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}\wedge a=0\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
.
Conclude
o_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\leq}}}o_{2}
for
(o_{1},o_{2})\in S_{4}\cup S_{5}\cup S_{6}\cup S_{7}
.
∎

Theorem 4
.

\mathbf{KF}
has exactly nine global orderings
(
see

1
(i)
and
3
).

Proof.

By Proposition
7
, GE spaces satisfy only one global ordering of

\mathbf{KF}
.
We verified by computer that each ordering in Figure
3

is satisfied by some space.
21
21

21

The minimal ED and EO spaces each satisfy
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}
; the ED space with base
\{\{w,x\}
,
\{w,x,y,z\}\}
does not, nor does the EO
space with base
\{\{x\}
,
\{x,y\}
,
\{x,y,z\}\}
.

Since the zero operator cannot appear in the extender of a partial order on
\mathbf{KF}
, all

-

of the table in Figure

fail.
The same is true of rows

since

in KD and OU spaces.
The result follows by Lemma

.
∎

3.2
The monoid
\mathbf{KFG}
.

We now show that
\mathbf{KFG}
has ten global collapses and
12
global orderings.

Lemma 14
.

(i)

f\hskip-0.5ptbg=g\implies bg=g\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg\implies bib=bi
.

Lemma 14.​

(ii)

g=ga\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg=f\hskip-0.5ptbga\iff bg=bga\implies f\hskip-1.0pti=0
.

Proof.

(i) Left-multiply
g=f\hskip-0.5ptbg
by
b
to get
bg=f\hskip-0.5ptbg=g
.
Left-multiply by
f\hskip-1.0pt
to get
f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg
.
By Lemma
4
(ii),
bib=bi(bg\vee bi)=bi(f\hskip-0.5ptbg\vee bi)\leq b(i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptbg\vee bi)=bi
.
(ii) Apply
bg=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
and
bg=bga\implies bg=bg\vee bga=f\hskip-1.0pt
.
∎

The next lemma holds by Lemma
8
(i).

Lemma 15
.

In non-GE spaces
,

bib=bi\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
,
hence
iA=\varnothing\implies bibA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
.

Theorem 5
.

\mathbf{KFG}
has exactly ten global collapses
(
see
Figure
4
).

Proof.

We verified by computer that ten exist and claim no further ones do.

Case

1
.
(|\mathbf{K^{0}}|=7)

Suppose
o=p
for some
o\in\mathbf{G^{0}}
and
p\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
Right-multiply by
i
to get
pi=0
.
Thus
p\in\{0,i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}
.
But then
o=oa
, contradicting
f\hskip-1.0pti\neq 0
by Lemma
14
(ii).
Conclude
o\neq p
for all
o\in\mathbf{G^{0}}
and
p\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
Lemma
14
(i) implies
|\mathbf{G^{0}}|=3
.

Case

2
. (ED
1,2
)
The Case
1
proof yields
|\mathbf{G^{0}}|=3
and
o\neq p
for all
o\in\mathbf{G^{0}}
and

p\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}\setminus\{f\hskip-0.5ptb\}
.
Have
g\neq f\hskip-0.5ptb
since
g=f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies bg=f\hskip-0.5ptb=g
and
bg\neq f\hskip-0.5ptb
since
bg=f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=ibg=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-0.5ptb=0
.
Thus
f\hskip-0.5ptbg=f\hskip-0.5ptb
is the only optional equation in
\mathbf{KFG^{0}}
.

Case

3
. (OU
1,2
)
The Case
1
proof yields
o\neq p
for all
o\in\mathbf{G^{0}}
and
p\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
Right-multiply
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt
by
g
to get
f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg
.
22
22

22

The minimal OU space satisfies

g=bg
; the OU space with base
\{\{w\}
,
\{x\}
,
\{w,x,y,z\}\}
does not.
For ED and EO examples see Theorem
4
.

Case

4
. (EO
1,2
)
The Case
1
proof yields
o\neq p
for all
o\in\mathbf{G^{0}}
and
p\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}\setminus\{f\hskip-0.5ptb\}
.
Lemma
16
and
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pt
imply
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg
.
Claim
1
:
f\hskip-0.5ptb=bg\implies bg=g
.
Suppose
f\hskip-0.5ptb=bg
.
Then
f\hskip-1.0pti=f\hskip-0.5ptba=bga
.
Hence
bg\wedge ga\leq bg\wedge bga=f\hskip-0.5ptb\wedge f\hskip-1.0pti=0
.
Since
bg\leq f\hskip-1.0pt=g\vee ga
, conclude
bg=g
.
Claim
2
:
f\hskip-0.5ptb=g\iff bg=g
.

(\Rightarrow)
Left-multiply by
b
and apply Claim
1
.

(\Leftarrow)
Since
bi=ib
we have
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq b=bg\vee ib
.
Hence
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq bg=g
.
Conversely
g\leq f\hskip-1.0pt=(bg\vee bi)\wedge(bga\vee bia)=(g\vee ib)\wedge(ga\vee aib)=(g\wedge aib)\vee(ib\wedge ga)
.
Since
g\wedge ga=0
we conclude
g\leq g\wedge aib\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb
.
Together with
f\hskip-0.5ptbg=bg
, Claims
1
and
2
imply
f\hskip-0.5ptb=g\iff bg=g\iff f\hskip-0.5ptb=bg\iff f\hskip-0.5ptb=f\hskip-0.5ptbg
.

Case

5
. (partition)
Apply
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KFG})
and Lemma
14
(i).

Case

6
. (discrete)
Apply
f\hskip-1.0pt=0
.
∎

Lemma 17
.

\begin{array}[c]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq\mathsf{id}\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq aibi\iff bg\leq aibi\iff g\leq aibi\emph{.}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&\mbox{If }(X,\mathcal{T})\mbox{ is not GE then }f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq aibi\emph{.}\\
\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&\mbox{If }(X,\mathcal{T})\mbox{ is ED or EO then }f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pti\emph{.}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

(i) Right-multiply
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}
by
g
to get
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq\mathsf{id}
.

(\Rightarrow)
Have
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-1.0pt=b\wedge(bga\vee bia)\leq b(bga)\vee f\hskip-0.5ptb=(f\hskip-0.5ptbga\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\vee f\hskip-0.5ptb
.
The result follows since
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
and
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq\mathsf{id}\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbga\leq a
.

(\Leftarrow)
By Lemmas
8
(iv) and
9
(v),
Proposition
2
(v), and right duality,

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-1.0ptib\implies f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-1.0ptib\wedge f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-0.5ptb\wedge f\hskip-1.0pti
.
Thus
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge a=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge ga\leq f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge bga=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge f\hskip-0.5ptbga\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\wedge f\hskip-1.0pti=f\hskip-1.0ptib
.
Since
f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\iff f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
by Lemma
13
, conclude
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge ag=(f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge a)g\leq f\hskip-1.0ptibg=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
.
Thus
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq g\leq\mathsf{id}
, giving us the equivalence.
Clearly
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq aibi\iff bg\ (=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\vee i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\leq aibi\implies g\leq aibi
.
Since
aibi
is closed,
g\leq aibi\implies bg\leq aibi
.
(ii) Have
(\Rightarrow)
by (i).

(\Leftarrow)
Since
(X,\mathcal{T})
is not GE,
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq biba=f\hskip-1.0ptiba\vee iba=f\hskip-0.5ptbi\vee abi=f\hskip-1.0ptib\vee abi\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee abi
.
But
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\wedge abi\leq(b\setminus i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt)\wedge abi=b\wedge(bi\vee aib)\wedge abi=(bi\vee f\hskip-0.5ptb)\wedge abi\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb
.
The result follows.
(iii) By (i),
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pti
.
Since
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-0.5ptb\vee f\hskip-1.0pti
, we conclude
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pti\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbg=f\hskip-0.5ptb\implies f\hskip-0.5ptb=f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq\mathsf{id}
by Lemma
16
and (i).
∎

Theorem 6
.

\mathbf{KFG}
has exactly
12
global orderings
(
see
Figures
1
(ii)
and
4
).

Proof.

We verified by computer that each ordering in Figures
1
(ii)
and
4
is satisfied by some space.
23
23

23

The minimal GE space
satisfies
bg\leq aibi
;
\mathbb{R}
under the usual topology does not (consider
A=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{1/n:n=1,2,\dots\})
.
The minimal KD and OU spaces each satisfy
f\hskip-0.5ptbg\leq\mathsf{id}
; the KD space with base

\{\{u\}
,
\{v\}
,
\{u,w\}
,
\{u,v,w,x\}
,
\{y,z\}\}
does not, nor does the OU space
with base
\{\{w\}
,
\{x\}
,
\{w,x,y,z\}\}
.

Since
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq bi\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq aib\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq f\hskip-1.0ptib\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-1.0ptib
, the inequalities

f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq bi
and
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq aib
fail in GE spaces.
All other inequalities in the
12
extenders besides those in
Table
7
fail by the
\mathbf{KFG}
analogue of the
Theorem
4
argument.
By Lemma
10
it remains only to show that for each space type, the
inequalities in Table
7
are equivalent.
This holds by Lemma
17
.
∎

), a natural partial order exists on the six Kuratowski
monoids by setting
\mathcal{O}_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}_{2}
if and only if there exists a monoid homomorphism from

\mathcal{O}_{2}
onto
\mathcal{O}_{1}
.
The
\mathbf{KFG}
analogue appears in Figure
5
.

5
,
12
,
26
,

47
,
72
,
106
,
129
,
134
,
134
,
134
local collapses and
5
,
12
,
28
,
61
,

131
,
262
,
459
,
614
,
657
,
666
local orderings of
\mathbf{KFG^{0}}
over all
(X,\mathcal{T})
such
that
|X|=n
.
These numbers suggest (imply?) that
\mathbf{KFG^{0}}
has exactly
134
local collapses but they
are inconclusive for local orderings.

4.1
Equations in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
.

There are too many local collapses to name, so we number them instead.

Definition 3
.

Let
\phi A

(\psi A)
be the number of the collapse of
\mathbf{K^{0}}

(\mathbf{KF})

in Table
8
that
A
satisfies
.

These so-called
\phi
-number
s
(
\psi
-number
s
)
also refer to their associated collapses
.

Theorem 7
.

\mathbf{KF}
has exactly
70
local collapses
(
see
Table
8
).

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq X
.
By Theorem 2.10 in GJ,
A
satisfies one of the
30
collapses of
\mathbf{K^{0}}
in
Table
8
.
As the authors point out, each extends uniquely to a local collapse of
\mathbf{K}
by adding
left duals, with one exception: When
bA
and
iA
are both clopen and unequal, the equation

bA=aiA

(\!{\iff}(bA=X\mbox{ and }iA=\varnothing))
may or may not hold.
These
31
local collapses contain every equation in
\mathbf{K}
a subset can satisfy.
Hence we need only find equations
o_{1}A=o_{2}A
that involve at least one operator in

\mathbf{F}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
o_{1}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
and
o_{2}\in\mathbf{F}
.
Note that
(bA\neq X\mbox{ and }iA\neq\varnothing)\implies o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
by
Table
2
under this assumption.

We need only consider
19
of the
30
local collapses of
\mathbf{K^{0}}
since there are

11
dual pairs.
Let
\Phi_{1}=\{4,

7,

11,

12,

13,

18,

20,

24,

26\}

and
\Phi_{2}=\{9,

14,

16,

22,

25,

28,

30\}
.
By Lemma
12
, for
\phi A\in\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}
the collapse of
\mathbf{F^{0}}

that
A
satisfies is determined by Proposition
2
(v)–(vi)
and Lemmas
8
(iii),
9
(v),
and
11
(see Table
8
).
This completes the proof for
\phi A\in\Phi_{1}
since
bA\neq X
and
iA\neq\varnothing
.

Suppose
\phi A\in\Phi_{2}
.
The proof is done for the case
(bA\neq X
and
iA\neq\varnothing)
.

Suppose
bA=X
.
Clearly
\phi A=30\implies\psi A=69
and
iA=\varnothing\implies\psi A=61
.
Suppose
iA\neq\varnothing
.
Table
8
covers
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
and Table
2
eliminates
the cases
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}},\,o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
and
o_{1}\in\mathbf{F^{0}},\,o_{2}\in a\mathbf{F^{0}}
.
In the case that remains,
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
and
o_{2}\in a\mathbf{F^{0}}
.
By Lemma
11
(xi) we can assume
o_{1}=\mathsf{id}
.

Case

1
.
(\phi A\in\{9,14,16,25\})

Suppose
A=o_{2}A
.
Table
3
implies
f\hskip-1.0ptao_{2}A\in\{\varnothing
,
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
,
f\hskip-1.0ptiA
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbA
,

f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
,
f\hskip-1.0ptibA
,
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\}
.
Hence
f\hskip-1.5ptA\neq f\hskip-1.0ptao_{2}A
by Table
8
.
Since this contradicts
f\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pto_{2}A=f\hskip-1.0ptao_{2}A
we conclude
A\neq o_{2}A
.

Case

2
.
(\phi A\in\{22,28\})

By Table
8
,
a\mathbf{F^{0}}\!A=\{a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA,X\}
.
Suppose
\phi A=22
.
Since
A
is neither open nor equal to
X
,
A\neq o_{2}A
.
If
\phi A=28
then
A=iA=a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
by Lemma
11
(xi).

In the only remaining case we have
bA\neq X
and
iA=\varnothing
.
Since
iA=\varnothing\implies bi=i
we get
\phi A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\{9
,
14
,
16
,
22
,
28\}
.
Clearly
\phi A=25\implies\psi A=60
and
\phi A=30\implies\psi A=70
.
This completes the proof for
\phi A\in\Phi_{2}
.

Since
bA=X\implies ibA=bA
and
iA=\varnothing\implies biA=iA
, it remains only to find all
possible equations
o_{1}A=o_{2}A
for
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
when
\phi A\in\{1,2,6\}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{n}A
stand for: “The set
A
satisfies exactly
n
of the equations

f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-0.5ptbi
,
f\hskip-1.0ptib=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbi=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.”
Note that
\mathcal{E}_{2}A
is impossible.
Let
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
be the sets defined in Lemma
12
.

Case

1
.
(\phi A=1)

By Lemma
11
,
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=0
is the only equation in
C_{1}\cup C_{2}
that
A

can satisfy.
By Proposition
2
(vi),
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.
Thus
\mathcal{E}_{0}A\implies\psi A=1
.
If
\mathcal{E}_{1}A
then
f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies\psi A=3
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbiA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies\psi A=4
,
and
f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
implies
\psi A=2
when
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing
,
\psi A=6
when
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
.
Clearly
\mathcal{E}_{3}A\implies\psi A=5
.

Case

2
.
(\phi A=2)

\mathcal{E}_{0}A
implies
\psi A=7
when
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq f\hskip-1.0ptiA
and
\psi A=9
when
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA
.
Suppose
\mathcal{E}_{1}A
.
Since
f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptibA
by Proposition
2
(v)
and
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-0.5ptbA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
by
Lemma
9
(iv) it follows that
f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies\psi A=8
.
By Proposition
2
(v) and Lemma
8
(iii),

f\hskip-0.5ptbiA=(f\hskip-1.0ptibA\mbox{ or }f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA)\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbA=f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA
.
It follows that
f\hskip-0.5ptbiA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies\psi A=10
and
f\hskip-1.0ptibA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
implies
\psi A=11
when

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing
,
\psi A=13
when
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
.
Clearly
\mathcal{E}_{3}A\implies\psi A=12
.

Case

3
.
(\phi A=6)

Let
o\in\{f\hskip-0.5ptb,f\hskip-1.0pti,f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\}
.
Claim
(f\hskip-0.5ptbA=f\hskip-1.0ptibA
and
f\hskip-1.0ptiA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
and
oA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA)\implies f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
.
The hypothesis implies
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
by
Proposition
2
(i).
Hence
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=(f\hskip-0.5ptbA\cup f\hskip-1.0ptiA)\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=(f\hskip-1.0ptibA\cup f\hskip-0.5ptbiA)\setminus f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
by
Lemma
8
(iii), giving us the claim.
Lemma
8
(iii) also implies
(f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq f\hskip-0.5ptbA\mbox{ or }f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq f\hskip-1.0ptiA)\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbA\neq f\hskip-1.0ptiA
.
Thus when
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\supsetneq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\supsetneq\varnothing
, the four combinations of
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-0.5ptb
and

f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti
being satisfied or not produce
\psi
-number
s
23
-
26
.
If
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
, then
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\supseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\neq\varnothing
by
Lemma
11
(iii); in this case the four combinations produce
\psi
-number
s

27
-
30
.
Finally,
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\implies\psi A=31
by Proposition
2
(vi) and
Lemma
8
(iii).

A space in which all
70

\psi
-number
s occur is given in the next section (see
Proposition
16
).
∎

Corollary 7
.

\mathbf{KF^{0}}
has exactly
62
local collapses.

Proof.

By Table
8
the only
\phi
-number
s that extend to multiple
\psi
-number
s with equal
collapses of
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
are the seven in the set
\Phi_{2}
above.
Since exactly one of them (
\phi
-number

25)
extends twice in this way, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 8
.

Columns
7
-
12
are correct in Table
8
.

Proof.

Except for
\psi
-number

61
,
k=14-2e
where
e
is the number of equal signs in the collapse of

\mathbf{K^{0}}
.
Similarly
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}=34-2e_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
where
e_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
is
e
plus the
number of equal signs in the remainder (see the footnote below
Table
8
).
Since
i=aba
the value of
\psi i
is determined by columns
5

(\psi a)
and
9

(\psi b)
.

The four closed
\phi
-number
s are characterized by their intersection with the set

\{\{bi,b\},\{bi,i\}\}
:
21
neither,
26

bi=b
,
29

bi=i
,
30
both.
Thus, since
bi(bA)=b(bA)\iff bibA=bA
and
bi(bA)=i(bA)\iff bibA=ibA
, the value of

\phi(bA)
is determined by the intersection of
\phi A
with
\{\{bib,b\},\{bib,ib\}\}
.
If
\phi(bA)\in\{21,26\}
we get
\psi(bA)
immediately.
Otherwise, since
i(bA)=\varnothing\!\iff\!ibA=\varnothing
,
b(bA)=X\!\iff\!bA=X
, and
bA=\varnothing\!\iff\!A=\varnothing
, the
value of
\psi(bA)
is determined by
\phi(bA)
and the intersection of
\psi(A)
with

\{\{ib,0\},\{b,1\},\{\mathsf{id},0\}\}
.

The value of
\psi(f\hskip-1.5ptA)
is determined similarly since
bi(f\hskip-1.5ptA)=b(f\hskip-1.5ptA)\!\iff\!bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.5ptA
,

bi(f\hskip-1.5ptA)=i(f\hskip-1.5ptA)\!\iff\!bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
,
i(f\hskip-1.5ptA)=\varnothing\!\iff\!i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
,
b(f\hskip-1.5ptA)=X\!\iff\!f\hskip-1.5ptA=X
, and

b(f\hskip-1.5ptA)=\varnothing\!\iff\!f\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
.

*Let
o\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
. If
oA=pA
for some
p\in\mathbf{KF}
besides
o
,
then
o=p
appears for at least one such
p
.
Since
o
and
ao
never both appear, it follows that
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(A)
equals
34
minus twice
the number of equal signs that appear.
Implied equations
oA=pA
hold either because
o=o^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}=p
both appear for some

o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
or because
o=ao^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}=ap
both appear for some
o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
.
The notation “
(x)
” either stands for
\phi
-number

x
or the remainder in
\psi
-number

x
.

Since
ig=0
we have
\psi(gA)\in\{37,44,48,56,60,61,67,70\}
.
Clearly
\psi(gA)=70\iff A=iA
.
Since
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptg=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
we have
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(gA)=X\iff i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=X
.
Hence
\psi(gA)=61\iff\psi A=61
.
Suppose
\psi(gA)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\{61,70\}
.
Since
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\iff f\hskip-1.0ptib(gA)=i(gA)
and
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\iff bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(gA)=i(gA)
we get
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing\iff\psi(gA)\in\{37,44\}
,
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\neq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\iff\psi(gA)\in\{48,60\}
, and
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\iff\psi(gA)\in\{56,67\}
.
Have
b(gA)=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(gA)=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies bibA=bA
by Lemma
11
(xii).
Thus
(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing\mbox{ and }bibA\neq bA)\implies\psi(gA)=37
and
(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\neq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\mbox{ and }bibA\neq bA)\implies\psi(gA)=48
.
By
\mbox{Ord}(\mathbf{KFG})
,
f\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\implies b(gA)=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(gA)
.
Thus
(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing\mbox{ and }f\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA)\implies\psi(gA)=44
and

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing\neq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.5ptA\implies\psi(gA)=60
.
Note that
(b(gA)=(gA)\mbox{ and }biA=iA)\implies A=gA\cup iA=bgA\cup biA=bA
.
It follows that
\phi A=23\implies\psi(gA)=56
.
Since
A=bA\implies gA=gbA=f\hskip-0.5ptbA\implies(gA)=b(gA)
it follows that
A=bA\neq iA\implies\psi(gA)=67
.
We verified by computer that in all remaining cases, both possible values of
\psi(gA)

occur.
∎

Table
8
shows that every even number from
2
to
34
occurs as the
value of
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(A)
for some
A
.
There are
16
self-dual
\psi
-number
s and
27
dual pairs.
The following
\psi
-number
s represent collapses of
\mathbf{KF}
that are both local and global:

1
(GE),
6
(KD),
38
(ED),
39
(OU),
40
(EO),
58
(P),
68
(D).

Non-Kuratowski spaces satisfy equations that exclude certain
\phi
-number
s.
Up to duality and excluding
\phi
-number

30
, the possibilities are:
ED
11
,
13
,

,

,

,

;

,

,

,

;
EO

,

,

;

25
;
D (none).

We verified that all
\psi
-number
s extended from these
\phi
-number
s occur, thus no
\psi
-number
is excluded
nontrivially from any non-Kuratowski space type.
However,
\psi
-number

61
is nontrivially excluded from KD spaces
(see Proposition
11
).
The possible
\psi
-number
s in KD spaces are thus:
6
,

,

,
31
,
38
-
40
,

-
60
,

62
-

.
We verified that each occurs in some KD space.

For each
n\leq 11
we computed the relative frequencies of
\psi
-number
s over all nonhomeomorphic
spaces on
n
points.
The results point strongly to
38
as the asymptotically rarest
\psi
-number
and
49
the
commonest (in a tie with its dual,
\psi
-number

50
) as
n\to\infty
.
The same conclusions hold for the corresponding
\phi
-number
s,
11
and
\{18,19\}
.
Table
9
shows the minimum space cardinality required for any given

\psi
-number
to occur.

Let
m\preceq n
if and only if
(A\subseteq X\mbox{ satisfies }\psi A=m)\implies(B\subseteq X\mbox{ exists with }\psi B=n)
.
We verified that the relation
\preceq
is contained in the partial order in
Figure
6
.
The two relations must surely be equal; entries with Boolean set operators in
Table
10
are posed to the reader as challenging exercises.

The next corollary formally justifies our use of the term
completely full
.

Corollary 9
.

Every completely full space is full
.

The converse is not true in general
.

Proof.

Using Table
8
it is easy to verify that
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))=4,10,16,20,22,34

implies
k((X,\mathcal{T}))\geq 2,6,8,10,10,14
, respectively, and in KD spaces
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(A)=28\implies\psi A=6\implies k(A)=14
.
Thus the first sentence holds.
The minimal non-indiscrete partition space is full but not completely full (see
Table
20
).
∎

The next corollary gives every pair
o_{1},o_{2}
in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
such that
ao_{1}A\neq o_{2}A
holds
in general.

Corollary 10
.

(i)

ao_{1}A\nsubseteq o_{2}A
if
o_{1},o_{2}\in{\downarrow}\{f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}
.

(ii)

ao_{1}A\neq o_{2}A
if
o_{1}=\mathsf{id}
and
o_{2}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}\setminus\{\mathsf{id}\}

or
o_{1},o_{2}
both belong to
\{i,ibi,bi\}
,

\{ib,bib,b\}
,
or

\{i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
.

Proof.

(i) Since the inclusions
a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\subseteq f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
imply
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=X
the result
holds by Lemma
11
(x).
(ii) Suppose
aA=oA
where
o\in\{i,ibi,bi\}
.
Since
iA\subseteq oA\subseteq aA\implies iA=\varnothing\implies oA=\varnothing\implies A=X\implies oA\neq\varnothing
we
conclude
aA\neq oA
.
The same holds for
o\in\{ib,bib,b\}
by a dual argument.

In the remaining cases we can assume
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
.
Suppose
ao_{1}A=o_{2}A
.
Then
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\cap ao_{2}A=\varnothing
.
Hence
o_{2}A=X
.
Thus
(\mathbf{K^{0}}o_{1})A=\{\varnothing\}
and
(\mathbf{K^{0}}o_{2})A=\{X\}
.
But
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}o_{2}
or
o_{2}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}o_{1}
, contradicting
X\neq\varnothing
.
∎

A topological space is connected if and only if it contains no subset with
\psi
-number

68
.
It is resolvable (see Hewitt
[
84
]
) if and only if it contains a subset with

\psi
-number

61
(otherwise it is irresolvable).
A non-clopen subset
A
is regular closed if and only if
\phi A=26
and regular open if
and only if
\phi A=27
.

4.2
Inclusions in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA

We now find all local orderings on
\mathbf{KF}
.

Definition 4
.

Suppose
A
satisfies a collapse
C
of
\mathcal{O}
and
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A

(o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{O})
.

If
C
implies the inclusion we call the inequality
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
a

base inequality
of
C
,
otherwise we call it
optional with respect to

C
and say it is
optionally satisfied
by
A
.

These definitions apply similarly to partial orders on
\mathcal{O}
.

Figure
7
gives all disjointness and base inequalities in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
for
each of the
70
local collapses of
\mathbf{KF}
.

Proposition 8
.

\mathbf{KF^{0}}
has exactly
274
local orderings
.

Proof.

We verified by computer that
274
occur.
Claim: (i) Whether
A
satisfies
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
is determined by
\psi A
and
the action of
I_{opt}
on
A
(see Table
11
).

Note
. It is easy to check that no inequality in
I_{opt}
is optional with
respect to any
\psi
-number

>50
, hence each generates only one local ordering on
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
(ii) Table
12
is correct.
(iii) If
\psi A\leq 50
every possible collection of inequalities in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
satisfied
optionally by
A
is represented by some combination of zero or one parenthesized lists
from column
1
with the same from column
2
in Table
13
.

(i) We can assume
o_{1},o_{2}
are neither disjoint nor comparable, for
o_{1}\leq o_{2}\implies o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
,
o_{2}\leq o_{1}\implies(o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\iff o_{1}A=o_{2}A)
,
(o_{1}\wedge o_{2}=0\mbox{ and }o_{1}A\neq\varnothing)\implies o_{1}A\nsubseteq o_{2}A
, and
o_{1}A=\varnothing\implies o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
.
The result follows by Table
11
.

(ii) We verified by computer that each non-blank entry in Table
12

occurs optionally and claim that no blank ones do.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\{f\hskip-1.0ptib,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-0.5ptbi\}
.

\psi

.

(v) when a subset satisfies one or more
equations in
\mathcal{O}
the inequalities in
\mathcal{O}
it satisfies are determined.
When no equations in
\mathcal{O}
are satisfied then either zero or two inequalities in
\mathcal{O}
are.

Since

it follows that

and
\psi A=9\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.

and
f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\nsubseteq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
.
It follows that
\psi A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\{2
,
3
-
5
,
8
,
10
-
12
,
15
,
17
-
19
,
22
,

26
,
28
-
30
,
33
,
36
,
37
,
43
,
44\}
. Since
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptibA
we have
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
by Proposition
2
(vii).
Thus
f\hskip-0.5ptbA=f\hskip-1.0ptibA\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\implies f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA
.
Hence
A
cannot have
\psi
-number

7
,
23
,
24
, or
27
since each contains
f\hskip-0.5ptb=f\hskip-1.0ptib
but
not
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=f\hskip-1.0pti
.
Since
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-0.5ptbA\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptiA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptibA
,
\psi A\neq 16
.
Thus all blank entries in row
5
are correct.

The implications
A\subseteq ibA\implies bibA=bA
and
ibA=bA\implies A\subseteq ibA
give us the
blank entries in row
6
.
Those in rows
7
-
9
follow from the equations
o=0
for
o\in\mathcal{O}
.
Since
f\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A\implies bA=A
we have
\psi A=50\implies(f\hskip-0.5ptbA\nsubseteq A\mbox{ and }f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\nsubseteq A)
.
Since
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A\implies f\hskip-1.0ptiA\subseteq A\implies biA\subseteq A\implies ibiA=iA
the other
eliminations in rows
10
and
11
follow from the equation
f\hskip-0.5ptb=0
and inequation

ibi\neq i
.

(iii) We leave cases with six or fewer combinations to the reader.

Case 1.

(\psi A=1)

We found above that
biA\subseteq ibA\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
and
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ibA\implies f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.
By Proposition
2
(ii) any two inclusions among
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq A
,

f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A
,
f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq A
imply the third.
Thus by Tables
11
and
12
all possible lists appear.
Suppose one list is selected from each column.
List 1 contains exactly one inequality
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
with
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{O}
.
It cannot be combined with the list
(o_{2}\leq\mathsf{id})
since then
o_{1}\leq\mathsf{id}
giving us
the whole row.
When
o_{2}=f\hskip-1.0ptib
the list
(f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id})
is similarly unavailable.
Since
o_{2}\in\{f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}
occurs four times and
o_{2}=f\hskip-1.0ptib
just once,
there are
(4\times 5)+(1\times 4)=24
possible combinations.
Clearly
12
combinations are possible when the empty list is selected from at least one
column, giving us a total of
36
possible combinations.

Case 2.

(\psi A=7)

We again get
12
combinations by selecting zero lists from one or both columns.
Suppose one list is selected from each column.
We claim that neither
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}
nor
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq\mathsf{id}
can be combined with
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
.
The latter is obvious since
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\neq\varnothing
.
We showed above that
\psi A=7\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA\nsubseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
.
Thus
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\nsubseteq a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptibA
by Proposition
2
(vii).
Hence the claim holds.
Since
(f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq A\mbox{ and }A\subseteq ibA)\implies f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq ibA\implies biA\subseteq ibA
the
claim implies that only one combination is possible when
A
satisfies
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
.
Since lists without
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
contain an inequality
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
as in Case
1
,
they each produce three combinations.
Thus
(3\times 3)+1=10
combinations are possible.

Case 3.

(\psi A=9)

Since
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0ptiA\iff f\hskip-0.5ptbA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
, if
A
satisfies
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}
it satisfies every
inequality in column
2
.
Selecting zero lists from at least one column clearly produces seven combinations.
Suppose one list is selected from each column.
Since
f\hskip-0.5ptbiA\subseteq ibA\iff biA\subseteq ibA
and
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\subseteq ibA\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibA=\varnothing
neither
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}
nor
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq\mathsf{id}
can be combined with
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
.
Since
(f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A\mbox{ and }A\subseteq ibA)\implies f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ibA
the inequality
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
therefore produces only one combination.
Hence only four are possible giving us a total of
11
.

Case 4.

(\psi A=14)

Since
f\hskip-1.0ptiA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
and
f\hskip-0.5ptbA\cup f\hskip-1.0ptiA\subseteq A\iff f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq A
it follows that combining
f\hskip-0.5ptb\leq\mathsf{id}
with either
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}
or
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}
produces
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}
.
Apply the Case
1
proof to get
11+20=31
possible combinations.

Case 5.

(\psi A=25)

Since
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-0.5ptbiA
and
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\neq\varnothing
the inequality
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
cannot be combined
with either
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq\mathsf{id}
or
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}
.
When it is combined with
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}
we get
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq ib
.
Note that
(f\hskip-0.5ptbi)
is the same as
(f\hskip-0.5ptbi,f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0ptib)
.
It follows that
11
combinations are possible.

Case 6.

(\psi A=27)

Since
f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
and
f\hskip-1.0ptibA\neq\varnothing
the inequality
\mathsf{id}\leq ib
cannot be combined
with either
f\hskip-1.0ptib\leq\mathsf{id}
or
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\leq\mathsf{id}
.
When it is combined with
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\leq\mathsf{id}
we get
bi\leq ib
.
It follows that
11
combinations are possible.
∎
