As one might guess,
\mathbf{KF^{0}}\!A
and
\mathbf{KF^{0}}(aA)
determine the local ordering of
\mathbf{KF}
that

A
satisfies.

Lemma 18
.

The partial order that
A
satisfies on
\mathbf{KF}
is determined by the partial
orders that
A
and
aA
satisfy on
\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq X
.
We claim every inclusion in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
that is optional with respect to
\psi A
is
equivalent to some inclusions in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}\!A
or some inclusions in
\mathbf{KF^{0}}(aA)
.
Let
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}\setminus\{0\}
.

Case 1.

(o_{1}A\subseteq ao_{2}A)

Optionality with respect to
\psi A
implies
o_{1},o_{2}
are neither disjoint nor
comparable.
The claim follows by Table
14
.

Case 2.

(ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A)

Have
ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\iff ao_{1}a(aA)\subseteq o_{2}a(aA)
.
Suppose
o_{1}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
.
Then
ao_{1}a\in\mathbf{K^{0}}
.
The claim follows since
o_{2}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}\implies o_{2}a\in a\mathbf{K^{0}}
(apply Case 1) and
o_{2}\in\mathbf{F^{0}}\implies o_{2}a\in\mathbf{F^{0}}
.
Suppose
o_{1}\in\{i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\}
.
Since Table
2
implies
bA=X
, the claim holds by
Lemma
11
(x).
The case
o_{1}\in{\downarrow}\{f\hskip-2.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.6pt\}
follows, for
ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\iff ao_{2}A\subseteq o_{1}A
and we have
o_{2}\in\mathbf{K^{0}}\cup\{i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt,f\hskip-1.0pt\}
by Corollary
10
.
∎

Exactly
496
local orderings of
\mathbf{KF}
are satisfied by subsets in spaces of cardinality

10
.
No further ones are satisfied in spaces of cardinality
11
; does this imply exactly

496
exist in general?
This may be a deep question.
Lacking such a proof, we instead proved it both directly and by applying
Proposition
8
and Lemma
18
.
The details are left to the reader (each proof is similar to the proof of
Proposition
8
).

Theorem 8
.

\mathbf{KF}
has exactly
496
local orderings
.

The next corollary follows easily by computer.

Corollary 11
.

\mathbf{K}
has exactly
66
local orderings
.

Given the
496
local orderings on
\mathbf{KF}
, the ordering under implication on all equations
and inclusions in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
is easily obtained by computer.
Those that are neither impossible nor hold in general can be described and counted as
follows.
Let
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathbf{KF^{0}}
.
We naturally select the equation
o_{1}A=o_{2}A
to represent both itself and
ao_{1}A=ao_{2}A
.
We also need consider only one of the two equations
o_{1}A=ao_{2}A
and
ao_{1}A=o_{2}A
.
Hence there are at most
2\times\binom{17}{2}=272
equations to consider.
Corollary
10
excludes
\binom{7}{2}+15=36
of them, reducing the total to

236
.
If
o_{1}\leq o_{2}
then
o_{1}A\subseteq ao_{2}A\iff o_{1}A=\varnothing
and
ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\iff o_{2}A=X
.
If
o_{1}\leq ao_{2}
then
ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A\iff ao_{1}A=o_{2}A
.
The only remaining inclusions in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
of the forms
ao_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
and
o_{1}A\subseteq ao_{2}A

are those where
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
are incomparable.
Up to left duality there are
90
such inclusions, eight of which are impossible by
Corollary
10
(i).
We similarly need consider only
90
inclusions of the form
o_{1}A\subseteq o_{2}A
.
Thus
236+172=408
relations suffice to represent all non-general equations and
inclusions that occur in
\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA
.

Corollary 12
.

The
408
representative relations described above break into
74
equivalence
classes under logical equivalence
(
see Table
15
).

Figure
8
gives their ordering under implication
.

Figure
9
is the operator analogue.

Duality accounts for the vertical symmetry in Figure
8
.
By coincidence, the number of relational classes it contains equals the number of local
collapses of
\mathbf{KF}
when both are counted up to duality (
43
).

5
The Interplay Between
\mathbf{\mathbf{KF}}
and

\mathbf{KF}\hskip-1.0ptA

5.1
Introduction.

Definition
3
.
3
and Lemma
3
.
15
in GJ are reprinted below.

Definition 5
.

(GJ, p
.

22
)

Let
\{(X_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i}):i\in I\}
be a family of topological spaces
.

The
sum space

\sum_{i\in I}(X_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i})
is the space on the
disjoint union
\dot{\cup}_{i\in I}X_{i}:=\cup_{i\in I}(X_{i}\times\{i\})
with base

\cup_{i\in I}\{S\times\{i\}:S\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\}
.

Lemma 19
.

(GJ, p
.

27
)

Let
\{(X_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i}):i\in I\}
be a family of spaces with the
X_{i}

pairwise disjoint and let
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
be two Kuratowski operators
.

Then
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
are equal on the sum space
\sum_{i\in I}(X_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i})
if and only if they are equal on each
(X_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i})
.

Furthermore if
S:=\dot{\cup}_{i\in I}S_{i}
is a subset of

\dot{\cup}_{i\in I}X_{i}
then
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
agree on
S
if and
only if they agree on each
S_{i}
.

Let
X_{n}:=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X,\mathcal{T})
for
n\geq 1
.
The following is Proposition
3
.
16
in GJ.

Proposition 9
.

(GJ, p
.

27
)

Let
(X,\mathcal{T})
have Kuratowski monoid
\mathbf{K}
.

(i)
If
K((X,\mathcal{T}))=2
then
(X,\mathcal{T})
is a full space
.

(ii)
If
K((X,\mathcal{T}))\in\{6,8\}
then
X_{2}
is full
with Kuratowski monoid
\mathbf{K}
.

(iii)
If
K((X,\mathcal{T}))=10
then
X_{3}
is full with
Kuratowski monoid
\mathbf{K}
.

(iv)
If
K((X,\mathcal{T}))=14
then
X_{4}
is full with
Kuratowski monoid
\mathbf{K}
.

As GJ point out, for
n\geq 2
Lemma
19
implies
X_{n}
has the same
Kuratowski monoid as
(X,\mathcal{T})
and
K((X,\mathcal{T}))>k(X_{n-1})\implies k(X_{n})>k(X_{n-1})
.
25
25

25

Since Lemma
19
applies to all GE operators
o_{1},o_{2}

we similarly have
K_{\!f}((X,\mathcal{T}))>k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(X_{n-1})\implies k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(X_{n})>k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(X_{n-1})
.

After proving Proposition
9
(iv) for the hypothetical case
k((X,\mathcal{T}))=6
and
noting that the sum space on two copies of the minimal Kuratowski space does not contain
a
14
-set, they write:

We do not know of a space where more than
three copies are required
,
or in fact any Kuratowski space with k-number

6
.

No such spaces exist.
We prove this and investigate the
\mathbf{KF}
analogues.
Several preliminary results are needed.

Lemma 20
.

(i)
t
If
A
and
iB
are closed then

i(A\cup B)=iA\cup iB
and
bi(A\cup B)=biA\cup biB
.

(ii)
t
If
A
and
bB
are open then

b(A\cap B)=bA\cap bB
and
ib(A\cap B)=ibA\cap ibB
.

(iii)
t
For all
B\subseteq X
,

bibA=ibA\implies ib(A\cup B)=ibA\cup ibB
and
ibiA=biA\implies bi(A\cap B)=biA\cap biB
.

Proof.

(i) The hypothesis implies
i(A\cup B)=(i(A\cup B)\cap A)\cup(i(A\cup B)\setminus A)\subseteq A\cup iB
.
Hence
i(A\cup B)=(i(A\cup B)\setminus iB)\cup(i(A\cup B)\cap iB)\subseteq iA\cup iB
.
The reverse inclusion holds in general.
The second equation follows.
(ii) is the dual of (i) and (iii) follows easily from (i) and (ii).
∎

Lemma 21
.

\begin{array}[c]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&\mbox{If }A\mbox{ and }B\mbox{ each satisfy }bib=b\mbox{ then so does }A\cup B\emph{.}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&\mbox{If }A\mbox{ and }B\mbox{ each satisfy }ibi=i\mbox{ then so does }A\cap B\emph{.}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

(i)
b(A\cup B)=bA\cup bB=bibA\cup bibB=b(ibA\cup ibB)\subseteq bi(bA\cup bB)=bib(A\cup B)
.
(ii) is the dual of (i).
∎

Lemma 22
.

\begin{array}[c]{r@{\hspace{3pt}}l}\emph{(i)}\hskip 3.0&ibA=ib(A\cap ibA)=ib(A\cap bibA)\emph{.}\\
\emph{(ii)}\hskip 3.0&A\cap ibA\mbox{ and }A\cap bibA\mbox{ each satisfy }bib=b\emph{.}\\
\emph{(iii)}\hskip 3.0&f\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)=(b(A\cap B)\setminus iA)\cup(b(A\cap B)\setminus iB)\emph{.}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

(i) Let
x\in ibA
and
U
be an open neighborhood of
x
.
Since
x\in bA
,
U\cap ibA\cap A\neq\varnothing
.
Thus
ibA\subseteq ib(A\cap ibA)\subseteq ib(A\cap bibA)\subseteq ibA
.
(ii) By (i) we have
b(A\cap ibA)\subseteq bA\cap bibA=bibA=bib(A\cap ibA)
.
The same applies to
A\cap bibA
.
(iii) Have
ba(A\cap B)=b(aA\cup aB)=aiA\cup aiB
.
The result follows.
∎

Lemma 23
.

(i)
t
If
A\cup B
satisfies
bib=bi

then
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ibB
.

(ii)
t
If
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
and
iB
are both empty
then
bi(A\cup B)=biA
.

(iii)
t
If
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
is empty and
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB
is
closed then
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gB)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gaB)
.

(iv)
t
If
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
is empty then
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB\subseteq i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
for all
B\subseteq X
.

Proof.

(i) Apply Lemma
4
(i) to get
ibA\subseteq bib(A\cup B)=bi(A\cup B)\subseteq b(iA\cup B)=biA\cup bB
.
Hence
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=ibA\setminus biA\subseteq bB
.
Thus
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subseteq ibB
.
(ii) Have
i(A\cup B)\subseteq bi(A\cup B)\subseteq b(A\cup iB)=bA
.
Thus
i(A\cup B)\subseteq ibA
.
Conclude
bi(A\cup B)\setminus biA\subseteq bibA\setminus biA\subseteq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
.
(iii) By Lemma
20
(i) we have
ib(A\cup gB)=i(bA\cup bgB)=ibA\cup i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB
.
Substitute
aB
for
B
to get
ib(A\cup gaB)=ibA\cup i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptaB=ib(A\cup gB)
.
By (ii),
bi(A\cup gB)=biA=bi(A\cup gaB)
.
(iv) Let
x\in i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB\cap biA
and
U
be an open neighborhood of
x
.
Have
\varnothing\neq U\cap i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB\cap iA\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptB
.
Thus
U\cap(A\cap B)\neq\varnothing\neq U\cap(A\setminus B)
.
Hence
x\in b(A\setminus B)\cap b(A\cap B)

\subseteq

(b(A\setminus B)\cup b(B\setminus A))

\cap

(ba(A\cup B)\cup b(A\cap B))=f\hskip-1.0pt(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
.
Thus
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptB\cap bi(aA)\subseteq f\hskip-1.0pt(aA\mathop{\triangle}B)=f\hskip-1.0pta(A\mathop{\triangle}B)=f\hskip-1.0pt(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
.
But
biA\cup bi(aA)=ai\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=X
.
∎

5.2
Interrelationships between the GE monoid and local collapses.

Obviously, dual
\phi
-number
s always occur together in any given space.
This also holds for two dual pairs.

Proposition 10
.

\phi
-number
s
18
-
21
(
equivalently
,

\psi
-number
s
49
-
52
)
always occur together in
any given space
.

Proof.

Suppose
\phi A=21
.
There exists a point
x\in biA\setminus iA
.
Let
B=A\setminus\{x\}
.
Since
iA\subseteq B
we have
x\in biA\subseteq bB\subseteq bA=A
.
Hence
bB=bA
.
Since
iA\subseteq a(\{x\})
we have
iA\subseteq ia(\{x\})
.
Thus
iB=i(A\setminus\{x\})=iA\cap ia(\{x\})=iA
.
It follows that
oB=oA
for all
o\in\mathbf{K^{0}}\setminus\{\mathsf{id}\}
.
Since
A=bA\supsetneq biA\supsetneq iA
we have
|A\setminus iA|\geq 2
.
Thus
B\neq iA=iB
.
Conclude
\phi B=19
.
Conversely
\phi A=19\implies\phi(bA)=21
by Table
8
.
∎

Corollary 13
.

k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))\neq 12
.

Proof.

By Table
8
,
\phi A\in\{20,21\}\!\iff\!k_{f}(A)=12
and

\phi A\in\{18,19\}\!\implies\!k_{f}(A)=14
.
Apply Proposition
10
.
∎

Lemma 24
.

Kuratowski and OU spaces always contain at least one non-clopen, regular closed subset,
i.e., a subset with
\phi
-number

26
.

Proof.

For some
A\subseteq X
,
bib(biA)=bi(biA)=biA\neq i(biA)=ib(biA)
.
Conclude
\phi(biA)=26
by Table
17
.
∎

Lemma 25
.

If
\phi A\in\{24,26\}
and
\psi B=61
then
\psi(A\cup B)=42
.

Proof.

Have
X=bB\subseteq b(A\cup B)
.
Thus
A\cup B
satisfies
ab=0
.
Have
iB=\varnothing
.
Thus by Lemma
4
(i),
ibi(A\cup B)\subseteq ib(A\cup iB)=ibA=iA=iA\cup iB\subseteq i(A\cup B)
.
Hence
A\cup B
satisfies
ibi=i=ab\vee i=a\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
Left-multiplying
ibi(A\cup B)=iA
by
b
yields
bi(A\cup B)=biA\neq iA=i(A\cup B)
.
By Table
17
we conclude
\psi(A\cup B)=42
.
∎

Proposition 11
.

KD spaces are irresolvable
.

Proof.

Suppose
(X,\mathcal{T})
is a Kuratowski space containing a subset
B
such that
\psi B=61
.
By Lemma
24
,
X
contains a subset
A
with
\phi
-number

26
.
By Lemma
25
we have
\psi(A\cup B)=42
.
Conclude
(X,\mathcal{T})
is not KD.
∎

Lemma 26
.

KD spaces always contain subsets
A\nsubseteq B
such that
\psi A=60
and

\psi B=62
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a KD space.
Claim
\psi A=60
for some
A\subseteq X
.
We have
bibE\neq biE
for some
E\subseteq X
.
Tables
3
and
15
imply
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(gE)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptE\neq\varnothing
.
Since
X
is KD and
gE
satisfies
i=0\neq i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
it follows by
Table
8
that
\psi(gE)\in\{48,60\}
.
The claim holds if
\psi(gE)=60
so we assume
\psi(gE)=48
.
Let
U=agE
.
Have
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
since
X
is KD.
Thus, since
abiU
is open and
bU=X
, Lemma
20
(ii) implies

b(U\setminus biU)=bU\cap babiU=aibiU=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptE=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptE\neq\varnothing
.
It follows that
U\setminus biU
satisfies
ib=b
and
\mathsf{id}\neq 0
.
Since we also have
i(U\setminus biU)=iU\cap iabiU=iU\setminus biU=\varnothing
, Table
8

and Proposition
11
imply
\psi(U\setminus biU)=60
.
Hence the claim holds.

By Lemma
24
there exists
V\subseteq X
such that
\psi V=62
.
If
A\nsubseteq V
we are done so we assume
A\subseteq V
.
Let
B=V\setminus bA
.
Note that
\psi(iaA)=68
.
Since
iV
and
biaA
are each open Lemma
20
(ii) implies
B=V\cap iaA=biV\cap biaA=b(iV\cap iaA)=bi(V\cap iaA)=biB
.
Since
V
is closed and
A\subseteq V
we have
bA\subseteq V
.
Hence
bA\subseteq iV
since
bA
is open.
Thus, since
V
is not open, we get
i(V\setminus bA)=iV\cap iabA=iV\setminus bA\subsetneq V\setminus bA
.
Conclude
\psi B=62
by Table
8
.
Since
A\cap B=\varnothing
the result follows.
∎

Lemma 27
.

If
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing
,

iA=ibiA\neq biA
,
and
bibA=bA
,
then

\psi A=31
.

Proof.

(i) By Table
8
,
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\neq\varnothing\implies(\psi A\in\{6,13,20,31,38\}
or
\phi A\in\{16,17,25\})
,
ibiA=iA\implies\psi A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\{6,13,38\}
,

ibiA\neq biA\implies\phi A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\{16,17,25\}
, and
bibA=bA\implies\psi A\neq 20
.
∎

Lemma 28
.

KD spaces always contain subsets with
\psi
-number
s
31
and
48
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a KD space.
By Lemma
26
there exist subsets
A\nsubseteq B
in
X
such that
\psi A=60
and

\psi B=62
.
Claim
\psi(A\cup B)=31
.
Since
iA
(
=\varnothing
) and
B
are closed, Lemma
20
(i) implies
i(A\cup B)=iA\cup iB=iB\neq biB=bi(A\cup B)
.
Hence
ibi(A\cup B)=ibiB=iB=i(A\cup B)
.
Lemma
21
(i) implies
bib(A\cup B)=b(A\cup B)
.
Since
ibA
and
B
are closed and
baA
and
aB
are open we have
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup B)=ib(A\cup B)\cap abi(A\cup B)=(ibA\cup iB)\cap ib(aA\cap aB)=(bA\cup iB)\cap ibaA\cap ibaB=bA\setminus B\neq\varnothing
.
Since
X
is KD,
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup B)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup B)
.
Hence the claim holds by Lemma
27
.
This implies
\psi(g(A\cup B))\in\{48,60\}
.
Have
g(A\cup B)=(A\cup B)\setminus i(A\cup B)=(A\cup B)\setminus iB=(A\setminus iB)\cup gB
.
Thus, since
gB\neq\varnothing
,
g(A\cup B)\cap B\neq\varnothing
.
Hence
ibg(A\cup B)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup B)=bA\setminus B\neq bg(A\cup B)
.
Conclude
\psi(g(A\cup B))=48
.
∎

Lemma 29
.

\psi A=37\implies\psi(A\cap ibA)=\psi(A\cap bibA)=44
.

Proof.

Have
i(A\cap ibA)\subseteq iA=\varnothing
.
Lemma
22
(i)-(ii) imply
b(A\cap ibA)=bib(A\cap ibA)=bibA\neq ibA=ib(A\cap ibA)
.
Thus
\psi(A\cap ibA)=44
by Table
8
.
The other proof is similar.
∎

None of the six possible Kuratowski monoids is characterized by the presence or absence
of a subset with any specific
\phi
- or
\psi
-number
.
However, one of the seven GE monoids is.

Proposition 12
.

A topological space is GE if and only if it contains a subset with
\psi
-number

44
.

Proof.

The “if” was established in Section
4
.
Conversely suppose
X
is GE.
Some
A\subseteq X
satisfies
f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptg=f\hskip-1.0pti\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt\neq 0
.
Since
gA
also satisfies
i=0
, Table
8
implies
\psi(gA)\in\{37,44\}
.
Apply Lemma
29
.
∎

Since Lemma
28
and Proposition
12
imply that
every Kuratowski space contains a subset
A
with
\psi A\in\{31,44\}
, no Kuratowski
space has
k
-number

6
.

Theorem 9
.

The GE monoid of a space implies it has subsets satisfying the following classes of dual

\psi
-number
s
:

discrete with
|X|>1
,

\{68\}
;

indiscrete partition
,

\{61\}
;

non-indiscrete partition
,

\{59,60\}
,

\{68\}
;

EO
,
ED
,

\{65,67\}
;

OU
,

\{62,63\}
,

\{65,67\}
;

KD
,

\{31\}
,

\{46,48\}
,

\{59,60\}
,

\{62,63\}
,

\{64,66\}
,

\{65,67\}
,

\{68\}
;

GE
,

\{42,44\}
,

\{62,63\}
,

\{65,67\}
.

Each list is sharp.

Proof.

Nonempty proper subsets of discrete (indiscrete) spaces have
\psi
-number

68

(61)
.
Every non-indiscrete partition space contains distinct points
x,y,z
such that
b(\{x\})\neq b(\{y\})=b(\{z\})
.
We have
\psi(\{x,y\})=60
and
\psi(b(\{x\}))=68
.
By Table
8
the boundary of every open set that is not closed has

\psi
-number

67
.
Thus every non-discrete, non-partition space contains a subset with
\psi
-number

67
.
Kuratowski and OU spaces contain a subset with
\psi
-number

62
by Lemma
24
.
GE spaces contain a subset with
\psi
-number

44
by Proposition
12
.
Lemma
28
implies KD spaces contain a subset with
\psi
-number

31
, the interior of
which has
\psi
-number

63
, and a subset with
\psi
-number

46
, the interior of which has
\psi
-number

64
.
Lemma
26
implies KD spaces contain a subset with
\psi
-number

60
, the closure of
which has
\psi
-number

68
.
We verified sharpness by computer.
∎

Table 2.1 in GJ points out that
\phi
-number
s
4
,
7
,
11
,
13
,
16
and their duals cannot
occur in connected spaces since they imply
iA\subsetneq ibiA=biA\subsetneq bA
and/or

iA\subsetneq ibA=bibA\subsetneq bA
.
Similarly,
\psi
-number
s
6
,
13
,
31
,
58
,
59
and their duals imply
\varnothing\subsetneq i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA\subsetneq bA
,
26
26

26

Since connected spaces do not admit
\psi
-number

6
,
Table
8
proves a conjecture of Moslehian and Tavallaii

[
124
]
that states every Kuratowski
14
-set generates
8

distinct sets under
\{f\hskip-1.0pt,i\}
in connected spaces.

\psi
-number
s
34
,
41
,
53
,
64
and
their duals imply either
\varnothing\subsetneq iA=biA\subsetneq bA
or
iA\subsetneq ibA=bA\subsetneq X
, and
\psi
-number

68
implies
\varnothing\subsetneq iA=bA\subsetneq X
.
We verified by computer that each of the remaining
\psi
-number
s occurs in at least one connected
space.

The next corollary holds by the above and Theorem
9
.

Corollary 14
.

KD
,
non-indiscrete partition
,
and discrete spaces of cardinality
>1
are
disconnected
.

There exist extremally disconnected spaces
,
i.e.
,
spaces that satisfy

ibi=bi
,
that are not disconnected
.

Proposition 13
.

Every KD space that contains a set
A
with
\psi A=39
contains a set

B
with
\psi B\in\{6,13\}
.

To prove this we need several preliminary results.

Lemma 30
.

Suppose
iA=\varnothing
in a KD space
X
.

Then
ibA\cap biB=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)
for all
B\subseteq X
.

Proof.

(\subseteq)
The hypotheses imply
ib(aA)=X
and
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
.
Note that
bibA
is open by Lemma
15
.
Thus by Lemmas
20
(ii),
4
(ii),
and
22
(iii),
ibA\cap biB=bi(bA\cap B)\subseteq bib(A\cap B)=b(ib(A\cap B)\cap ib(aA))=bi(b(A\cap B)\setminus iA)\subseteq bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)
.

(\supseteq)

i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)\subseteq ib(A\cap B)\subseteq ibA\cap ibB
.
∎

Lemma 31
.

Suppose
bibA=biA\neq bA
in a KD space
X
.

If
A\mathop{\triangle}gB
and
A\mathop{\triangle}gaB
each satisfy
bib=b
then
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gB)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gaB)\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

Since
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=\varnothing
and every subset satisfies
bi\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt
the equation holds by
Lemma
23
(iii).
Suppose
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gB)=\varnothing
.
By Lemmas
15
and
23
(ii),
b(A\cap agB)\subseteq b(A\mathop{\triangle}gB)=bib(A\mathop{\triangle}gB)\subseteq bib(A\cup gB)=bi(A\cup gB)=biA
.
Similarly
b(A\cap agaB)\subseteq biA
.
Since
gB\cap gaB=\varnothing
we have
agB\cup agaB=X
.
Thus
bA=b((A\cap agB)\cup(A\cap agaB))\subseteq biA
.
Conclude
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gB)\neq\varnothing
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

bibA=ibA\implies bi(aA)\cap biB\cap bi(aB)\subseteq bi(A\mathop{\triangle}B)\setminus ib(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
.

Proof.

Since
bi(aA)
is open, every open neighborhood
U
of
x\in bi(aA)\cap biB\cap bi(aB)

satisfies
i(A\mathop{\triangle}B)

=

i(A\cup B)\cap i(aA\cup aB)

\supseteq

U\cap i(aA)\cap iB

\neq

\varnothing

\neq

U\cap i(aA)\cap i(aB)

\subseteq

i(A\cup aB)\cap i(aA\cup B)

=

ia(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
.
∎

Lemma 33
.

If
iA=\varnothing
and
bibA=ibA
in a KD space
X
then
f\hskip-1.0ptibB\subseteq f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\mathop{\triangle}B)\cap f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\cup B)
for all
B\subseteq X
.

Figure 10:
The meet semilattices of
\phi
- and
\psi
-numbers under set inclusion.

Note that
\phi_{1}\subseteq\phi_{2}\implies\phi_{1}\leq\phi_{2}
; the same is
true of
\psi
-number
s.

Proof.

Let
x\in f\hskip-1.0ptibB
.
Have
ibA\cap biB=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cap B)
by Lemma
30
.

.

.
Thus
x\in bi(aA)
.

by Lemma

(iv),

x\in f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
by Lemma

.

x\in biB\subseteq bi(A\cup B)
.
Since
bi(aA)
is open by Lemma

, Lemma

(ii) implies
x\in bi(aA)\cap bi(aB)=bi(aA\cap aB)=aib(A\cup B)
.
∎

Corollary 15
.

In KD spaces
,

(\psi A=39

.

and

f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\mathop{\triangle}B)\neq\varnothing
.
The result follows by Table
8
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
13
.
Since Table
8
implies
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}(A)=20\implies\psi A=39
in KD spaces,
it follows that no KD space has
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
-number
20
.

Proof.

Suppose
X
is KD and
\psi A=39
for some
A\subseteq X
.
By Theorem
9
,
\psi E=\psi(aE)=31
for some
E\subseteq X
.
Have
\psi(gE),\psi(gaE)\in\{48,60\}
.
Hence
\psi(A\mathop{\triangle}gE),\psi(A\mathop{\triangle}gaE)\in\{6,13,20,31\}
by
Corollary
15
.
We can assume that
\psi(A\mathop{\triangle}gE)=\psi(A\mathop{\triangle}gaE)=31
.
Lemma
31
implies
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gE)=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0pt(A\cup gaE)\neq\varnothing
.
Since
ibiA\neq iA
, Lemma
21
(ii) implies
A\cup gE
and
A\cup gaE

cannot both satisfy
ibi=i
.
Suppose
A\cup gE
satisfies
ibi\neq i
.
Since
\psi(gE)\in\{48,60\}
we have
f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\cup gE)\neq\varnothing
by Lemma
33
.
Add
ibi\neq i
to the Corollary
15
argument to get
\psi(A\cup gE)\in\{6,13\}
.
Similarly,
\psi(A\cup gaE)\in\{6,13\}
if
A\cup gaE
satisfies
ibi\neq i
.
∎

Theorem 10
.

Table
18
lists the values of
k((X,\mathcal{T}))
and
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))
that
occur for each GE monoid
.

Proof.

We verified by computer that each combination occurs.
The rest are excluded by Table
8
, Corollary
13
,
Theorem
9
, and Proposition
13
.
∎

The largest
k
-number
(
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}
-number
) in each row of Table
18
is the space type’s

K
-number
(
K_{\!f}
-number
) since full (completely full) spaces of each type exist.

Table
19
lists named spaces satisfying some of the possible space type
and
k
-number
combinations (proofs are left to the reader).
Except for the Sierpiński space, each space with
k
-number

4
is cited in GJ’s proof of
Theorem 2.1.

5.3
Topological sums.

Lemma
19
implies the following corollary.

Corollary 16
.

The collapse of
\mathbf{K^{0}}

(\mathbf{KF})
satisfied by
A_{1}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}A_{2}
in
X_{2}
is the
intersection of the collapses of
\mathbf{K^{0}}

(\mathbf{KF})
satisfied by
A_{1}
and

A_{2}
in
(X,\mathcal{T})
.

The poset under set inclusion of all
\phi
-number
s
(
\psi
-number
s
)
is thus a meet semilattice

(
see Figure
10
).

Tables
21
and
22
list all

\phi
-
and
\psi
-number
s of disjoint unions
A_{1}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}A_{2}
in
X_{2}
given
those of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in
(X,\mathcal{T})
.

Note that
\alpha(A_{1}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}A_{2})\leq\min\{\alpha(A_{1}),\alpha(A_{2})\}
for

\phi,\psi
in place of
\alpha
.

As we noted earlier, GJ showed that adding one copy to a non-full sum of copies of a
given space strictly increases the sum’s
k
-number
.
We show in Corollary
17
below that it always increases by
2

or
4
.
The proof calls for several preliminary results.

Lemma 34
.

If
\phi A\in\{16,17\}
and
A\cup B
or
A\cap B
satisfies

bib=bi
then
biB\setminus ibB\subseteq bi(A\mathop{\triangle}B)\setminus ib(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\phi A\in\{16,17\}
and
A\cup B
satisfies
bib=bi
.
Let
x\in biB\cap bi(aB)
.
Have
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.0ptA\subseteq ibB
by Lemma
23
(i).
Thus
bi(aB)\subseteq biA\cup bi(aA)
.
Since
aA\mathop{\triangle}B=a(A\mathop{\triangle}B)
and
bi\setminus ib=bia\setminus iba
the result follows by
Lemma
32
.
The remaining case holds since
aA\cup aB
satisfies
bib=bi
when
A\cap B
does and

aA\mathop{\triangle}aB=A\mathop{\triangle}B
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

(i)
If
bibB=biB
and
A,A\cap B
each satisfy
f\hskip-1.0ptib=0
then

f\hskip-1.0ptibB\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbi(A\cup B)\cap f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\cup B)
.

Lemma 35.​

(ii)
If
bibB=biB
and
A,A\cup B
each satisfy
f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0
then

f\hskip-0.5ptbiB\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbi(A\cap B)\cap f\hskip-1.0ptib(A\cap B)
.

Proof.

(i)
bibB=biB\implies f\hskip-1.0ptibB=f\hskip-0.5ptbiB\subseteq biB\subseteq bi(A\cup B)
.
Let
x\in f\hskip-1.0ptibB
.
Since
bib(A\cap B)=ib(A\cap B)\subseteq ibB
, Lemma
4
(ii) implies

U\cap ibA\cap iB=U\cap i(bA\cap B)\subseteq U\cap ib(A\cap B)=\varnothing
for some open neighborhood

U
of
x
.
Since
x\in bibB=biB
, it follows that
x\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}ibA\cup ibB=ib(A\cup B)
by
Lemma
20
(iii).
Apply Lemma
8
(iv) to get the result.
(ii) is the dual of (i).
∎

Lemma 36
.

Suppose
A\cup B
and
A\cap B
each satisfy
ibi=i
.

If
biB=bB
then
ibiA\setminus iA\subseteq f\hskip-0.5ptbi(A\cap B)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in ibiA\setminus iA
.
Since
ibi(A\cap B)\subseteq i(A\cap B)\subseteq iA
,
x\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}ibi(A\cap B)
.
Since
x\in b(aA)
and
ibiA\subseteq biA\cap ibi(A\cup B)\subseteq i(A\cup B)\subseteq A\cup B\subseteq A\cup biB
,
U\cap ibiA\cap i(A\cap B)\neq\varnothing
for every open
U
containing
x
.
∎

Proposition 14
.

k((X,\mathcal{T}))\geq 10
if
\phi A=16
and
\phi B=26
for some

A,B\subseteq X
.

Proof.

Let
E=A\mathop{\triangle}B
.
Have
ibE\nsubseteq biE
by Lemma
23
(iv).
Suppose
A\cup B
or
A\cap B
satisfies
bib=bi
.
Then
biE\nsubseteq ibE
by Lemma
34
.
Hence
ibE,biE,ibiE,bibE
are pairwise distinct.
By Table
8
this implies
E
is neither open nor closed.
Thus
k(E)\geq 10
.
Suppose
A\cup B
and
A\cap B
both satisfy
bib\neq bi
.
Since they both satisfy
ibi\neq ib
, if either additionally satisfies both
f\hskip-1.0ptib\neq 0

and
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\neq 0
then it has
k
-number

\geq 10
by the argument above.
Hence we can assume each satisfies
f\hskip-1.0ptib=0
or
f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0
.
Since
f\hskip-1.0ptibB=f\hskip-0.5ptbiB\neq\varnothing
, Lemma
35
implies
A\cup B

satisfies
f\hskip-0.5ptbi\neq 0
and
A\cap B
satisfies
f\hskip-1.0ptib\neq 0
.
Hence
A\cap B
satisfies
f\hskip-0.5ptbi=0
.
It follows by Lemma
36
that
A\cup B
and
A\cap B
do not both
satisfy
ibi=i
.
Thus
|\{\mathsf{id},i,ibi,ib,bib\}(A\cap B)|=5
or
|\{\mathsf{id},i,ibi,bi,bib\}(A\cup B)|=5
.
∎

Corollary 17
.

If
(X,\mathcal{T})
is full then so is
X_{n}
for all
n
.

If
X_{n}
is not full then
2\leq k(X_{n+1})-k(X_{n})\leq 4
.

Proof.

The first assertion holds by Lemma
19
.
Suppose
X_{n}
is not full.
Some
A\subseteq X_{n}
then satisfies
k(A)=k(X_{n})<K(X_{n})=K((X,\mathcal{T}))
.
For some
B\subseteq X
Kuratowski operators
o_{1},o_{2}
exist such that
o_{1}A=o_{2}A
and

o_{1}B\neq o_{2}B
.
By Lemma
19
,
k(A\mathop{\dot{\cup}}B)\geq k(A)+2
in
X_{n+1}
.
Hence
k(X_{n+1})\geq k(X_{n})+2
.

It remains to show that
k(X_{n+1})\leq k(X_{n})+4
.
Since
k(X_{n+1})\leq K(X_{n+1})=K(X_{n})
we are done if
K(X_{n})\leq k(X_{n})+4
so
we assume
K(X_{n})\geq k(X_{n})+6
.
Theorem
10
implies
K(X_{n})=k(X_{n})+6
.
Since
X_{n}
is not full, if
n\geq 2
we have
k(X_{n})\geq k(X_{n-1})+2
, hence

K(X_{n-1})=K(X_{n})=k(X_{n})+6\geq k(X_{n-1})+8
.
Theorem
10
disallows this, so
n=1
.
By Theorem
10
only two cases are possible.

Case

1
.
(K((X,\mathcal{T}))=10)

Since ED spaces do not admit
\phi
-number
s
26,27
and OU spaces do not admit
\phi
-number

25
it
follows from Table
8
and columns
25
-
30
in
Table
21
that
k((X,\mathcal{T}))\geq 6
.

Case

2
.
(K((X,\mathcal{T}))=14)

By Table
8
and columns
13
-
30
in
Table
21
,
k(X_{2})=14
only if
X
contains a subset with
\phi
-number

16
.
It follows by Lemma
24
and Proposition
14
that
k((X,\mathcal{T}))\geq 10
.

Since both cases contradict
K((X,\mathcal{T}))=k((X,\mathcal{T}))+6
the result follows.
∎

Corollary 18
.

If
(X,\mathcal{T})
is completely full then so is
X_{n}
for all
n
.

If
X_{n}
is not completely full then
2\leq k_{f}(X_{n+1})-k_{f}(X_{n})\leq 20
.

Proof.

Adjust the proof above for
\mathbf{KF}
to get the first sentence and lower bound.
Since
k_{\hskip-0.5ptf}((X,\mathcal{T}))=10\implies k((X,\mathcal{T}))\leq 8
(see Table
8
),
Corollary
17
and Theorem
10
imply the
upper bound.
∎

Let

Table
20
follows directly from Theorem
9
and
Corollary
16
.
It is complete since no further
\psi
-number
s appear in
X_{n}
for
n\geq 4
.
Propositions
9
and
15
follow directly from
Theorem
9
, Lemma
19
, its
\mathbf{KF}
analogue, and
Table
20
.
The number of copies in each sum is sharp by Table
20
.

Proposition 15
.

(X,\mathcal{T})
have GE monoid
\mathbf{KF}
.

t

K_{\!f}((X,\mathcal{T}))\in\{4,16\}

X_{2}
is completely full with GE monoid
\mathbf{KF}
.

t
If
K_{\!f}((X,\mathcal{T}))\in\{10,20,22,28,34\}
then

X_{3}
is completely full with GE monoid
\mathbf{KF}
.

Our final result requires a computer to verify.

Proposition 16
.

There exists a topological space in which all
70

\psi
-number
s occur
.

Proof.

Let
X
be the
11
-point set
\{p,q,r,s,t,u,v,w,x,y,z\}
.
Resolvable topologies
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{4}
exist on

X
with the following property: For each
1\leq n\leq 68
there exists
A_{n}\subseteq X
such
that
\psi A_{n}=n
in
(X,\mathcal{T}_{m(n)})
for some
1\leq m(n)\leq 4
.
27
27

27

Bases
for
\mathcal{T}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{T}_{4}
are, respectively:

\{\{p,q\}
,
\{r,s\}
,
\{t,u\}
,
\{v,w\}
,
\{p,q,r,s,x\}
,
\{v,w,y\}
,

\{p,q,t,u,v,w,y,z\}\}
,
\{\{p,q\}
,
\{r,s\}
,
\{t,u\}
,
\{v,w\}
,
\{r,s,x\}
,

\{v,w,y\}
,
\{t,u,v,w,y,z\}\}
,
\{\{p,q\}
,
\{r,s\}
,
\{t,u\}
,
\{p,q,r,s,t,u,v\}
,

\{p,q,r,s,w\}
,
\{p,q,x\}
,
\{r,s,y\}
,
\{t,u,z\}\}
,
\{\{p,q\}
,
\{r,s\}
,

\{t,u\}
,
\{p,q,r,s,v\}
,
\{p,q,t,u,w\}
,
\{p,q,x\}
,
\{r,s,t,u,y\}
,
\{r,s,z\}\}
.
We verified by computer that
\mathcal{T}_{j}
generates
\psi
-number

61
for
j=1,2,3,4
;

\mathcal{T}_{1}
generates
\psi
-number
s
5
-
19
and
24
-
30
except
6
and
13
;

\mathcal{T}_{2}
generates
\psi
-number
s
6
,
13
,
20
-
22
, and
31
-
68
except
57
;

\mathcal{T}_{3}
generates
\psi
-number
s
2
-
4
and
57
; and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
generates
\psi
-number
s
1

and
23
.
For
j=4,\dots,11
, respectively, the largest number of
\psi
-number
s satisfied in one space on

j
points is
12,17,25,32,38,43,52,59
.
The smallest two-step increase is
11
, thus the smallest cardinality admitting all
70

is likely to be
13
.
Since there are approximately
16.5
billion nonhomeomorphic non-
T_{0}
spaces on
13

points, the probability of finding such a space using a home computer is very low at
present (Kuratowski
14
-sets do not occur in finite
T_{0}
spaces

[
83
]
).
Ruling out cardinality
12
might be even more challenging.
The smallest cardinality admitting all
30

\phi
-number
s is
10
.
One such
10
-space has base
\{\{q\}
,
\{r\}
,
\{s\}
,
\{t,u\}
,
\{v,w\}
,

\{q,x\}
,
\{r,s,y\}
,
\{q,v,w,x,z\}\}
.

For each
n
such that
\psi
-number

n
is a subset of
\psi
-number

69
, let
U_{n}
be the disjoint
union of three copies of
X
in positions
j\neq m(n)
with
A_{n}
in the
m(n)
th
position.
For all other
n
except
n=61
define
U_{n}
similarly with
\varnothing
in place of
X
.
By resolvability there exist
Q_{j}\subseteq X
such that
\psi Q_{j}=61
in
(X,\mathcal{T}_{j})

for
1\leq j\leq 4
.
Let
U_{61}=Q_{1}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}Q_{2}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}Q_{3}\mathop{\dot{\cup}}Q_{4}
.
Lemma
19
implies
\psi U_{n}=n
in
\sum_{j=1}^{4}(X,\mathcal{T}_{j})
for

1\leq n\leq 68
.
∎

Closing Remarks

Section 4 of GJ deals with the
Kuratowski
\mathcal{O}
-problem
: Does the set

\{|\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptA|

:
all
(X,\mathcal{T})
and all
A\subseteq X\}
have a finite supremum and if so what is it?
We generalize this problem by calling any optimization problem that involves a given

collection

\mathcal{O}
of set operators on the power set of a
space

X

defined in terms of a
system

\mathcal{S}\subseteq 2^{X}
satisfying certain
properties a
Kuratowski–Zarycki
(or
KZ
)
problem
.
A
seed

A
and/or
family

\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.0ptA
may also be involved.

Associated with every Kuratowski
\mathcal{O}
-problem is the KZ problem that asks to minimize
the cardinality of a
space
that maximizes the
family
.
GJ showed the answers are
14
and
9
, respectively, for
\mathcal{O}=\{b,i,\vee,\wedge\}
and

\{ia,\vee,\wedge\}
(see Propositions 4.3 and 4.4) and cited Moser
[
123
]
,
Herda and Metzler
[
83
]
, and Anusiak and Shum

[
8
]
for proving the answers of
9
,
7
, and
6
for
\mathcal{O}=\{b,i,\vee\}
,
\{a,b\}
, and
\{a,
generalized closure
\}
, respectively.
28
28

28

KZ
problems that minimize the space can be solved by a present-day home computer when the
answer is
\leq 11
.

In closure spaces, Soltan
[
156
]
addressed the above KZ problem for various

\mathcal{O}\subseteq\{a,b,i,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
as well as the one that minimizes the cardinality of a

system
that maximizes the
family
(for
\{a,b\}
the answer is
14
and
for
\{a,b,f\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt\}
it is
24
).
Bowron
[
23
]
minimized the cardinality of a
seed
that
maximizes the
family
for
\mathcal{O}=\{a,b\}
in topological spaces (the answer is
3
).

The following conjecture is supported by computer results:
every ED space contains
a nonempty subset
A
such that
bA=i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA
.
If this is true, then every connected ED space contains a set
A
such that
i\hskip-1.0ptf\hskip-1.5ptA=X
.

Table 22:

\psi
-number
s not both
\leq 30
.

Our original goal was to prove or disprove that no Kuratowski space has
k
-number

6
.
Shortly after finding a proof,
29
29

29

Our first proof does not involve the
boundary operator.
It can be found at
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/325995
.

the arXiv preprint of Canilang et al
.

[
29
]

led us to investigate similar questions about
\mathbf{KF}
.

Figure
11
gives a graphical summary
of the first century of literature related to the closure-complement theorem.

Banakh et al
.
(2018)

,
